    *   [4.7 Boko Haram continued to increase its presence in northern Cameroon. On 16 May, ten Chinese workers were abducted in a raid on a construction company camp in Waza, near the Nigerian border. Vehicles and explosives were also taken in the raid, and one Cameroonian soldier was killed. Cameroon's anti-terrorist Rapid Intervention Battalion attempted to intervene but were vastly outnumbered.[188] In July, the deputy prime minister's home village was attacked by around 200 militants; his wife was kidnapped, along with the Sultan of Kolofata and his family. At least 15 people, including soldiers and police, were killed in the raid. The deputy prime minister's wife was subsequently released in October, along with 26 others including the ten Chinese construction workers who had been captured in May; authorities made no comment about any ransom, which the Cameroon government had previously claimed it never pays.[189] In a separate attack, nine bus passengers and a soldier were shot dead and the son of a local chief was kidnapped. Hundreds of local youths are suspected to have been recruited. In August, the remote Nigerian border town of Gwoza was overrun and held by the group. In response to the increased militant activity, the Cameroonian president sacked two senior military officers and sent his army chief with 1000 reinforcements to the northern border region.[190][191][192]](#Boko_Haram_continued_to_increase_its_presence_in_northern_Cameroon._On_16_May,_ten_Chinese_workers_were_abducted_in_a_raid_on_a_construction_company_camp_in_Waza,_near_the_Nigerian_border._Vehicles_and_explosives_were_also_taken_in_the_raid,_and_one_Cameroonian_soldier_was_killed._Cameroon's_anti-terrorist_Rapid_Intervention_Battalion_attempted_to_intervene_but_were_vastly_outnumbered.[188]_In_July,_the_deputy_prime_minister's_home_village_was_attacked_by_around_200_militants;_his_wife_was_kidnapped,_along_with_the_Sultan_of_Kolofata_and_his_family._At_least_15_people,_including_soldiers_and_police,_were_killed_in_the_raid._The_deputy_prime_minister's_wife_was_subsequently_released_in_October,_along_with_26_others_including_the_ten_Chinese_construction_workers_who_had_been_captured_in_May;_authorities_made_no_comment_about_any_ransom,_which_the_Cameroon_government_had_previously_claimed_it_never_pays.[189]_In_a_separate_attack,_nine_bus_passengers_and_a_soldier_were_shot_dead_and_the_son_of_a_local_chief_was_)

From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

African jihadist organisation

Not to be confused with [Islamic State – West Africa Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_%E2%80%93_West_Africa_Province "Islamic State – West Africa Province").

**Jama'at Ahl al-Sunna li al-Da'wa wa al-Jihad**

**Boko Haram**,[[a]](#cite_note-19) officially known as **Jama'at Ahl al-Sunna li al-Da'wa wa al-Jihad**[[19]](#cite_note-20) (**JAS**)[[b]](#cite_note-22) and sometimes referred to as it **[Daular Musulunci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Boko_Haram "Territory of Boko Haram")**,[[21]](#cite_note-23)[[22]](#cite_note-24) is a [jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadist "Jihadist") militant organisation based in northeastern [Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigeria "Nigeria") and also active in [Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad "Chad"), [Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger "Niger"), northern [Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon "Cameroon"), and [Mali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mali "Mali").[[23]](#cite_note-Bureau-25)

Founded by [Mohammed Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Yusuf_(Boko_Haram) "Mohammed Yusuf (Boko Haram)") in 2002, the group was led by [Abubakar Shekau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Shekau "Abubakar Shekau") from 2009 until his death in 2021 during the [Battle of Sambisa Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sambisa_Forest_(2021) "Battle of Sambisa Forest (2021)"), although it splintered into other groups after Yusuf's death in 2009, as well as in 2015.[[24]](#cite_note-enc-26) When the group was first formed, their main goal was to "purify" the [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam "Sunni Islam") in [northern Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Nigeria "Northern Nigeria"),[[25]](#cite_note-27) believing jihad should be delayed until the group was strong enough to overthrow the [Nigerian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_government "Nigerian government").[[26]](#cite_note-too-much-2017-291-28) The group formerly aligned itself with the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant "Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant").[[27]](#cite_note-BokoHaramIS-29)[[28]](#cite_note-BokoHaramIS2-30) The group has been known for its brutality,[[29]](#cite_note-Matfess2017-31) and since the [insurgency started in 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boko_Haram_insurgency "Boko Haram insurgency"), Boko Haram has killed tens of thousands of people, in frequent attacks against the police, armed forces and [civilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilian "Civilian"). The conflict has resulted in the deaths of more than 300,000 children[[30]](#cite_note-32) and has [displaced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_displacement "Forced displacement") 2.3 million from their homes.[[31]](#cite_note-AP-18-11-15-33) Boko Haram has contributed to regional food crises and [famines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine "Famine").[[29]](#cite_note-Matfess2017-31)

After its founding in 2002, Boko Haram's increasing [radicalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radicalisation "Radicalisation") led to the suppression operation by the [Nigerian military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_military "Nigerian military") and the killing of Yusuf in July 2009.[[32]](#cite_note-34) Its unexpected resurgence, following a mass [prison break](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prison_breaks_in_Nigeria "Prison breaks in Nigeria")[in September 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauchi_prison_break "Bauchi prison break") in [Bauchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauchi_(city) "Bauchi (city)"), was accompanied by increasingly sophisticated attacks, initially against [soft targets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_target "Soft target"), but progressing in 2011 to include [suicide bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_bombing "Suicide bombing")[of police buildings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Abuja_police_headquarters_bombing "2011 Abuja police headquarters bombing")[and the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Abuja_United_Nations_bombing "2011 Abuja United Nations bombing") office in [Abuja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abuja "Abuja"). The government's establishment of a [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_emergency "State of emergency") at the beginning of 2012, extended in the following year to cover the entire northeast of Nigeria, led to an increase in both security force abuses and militant attacks.[[33]](#cite_note-35)[[24]](#cite_note-enc-26)[[34]](#cite_note-hundreds-36)[[35]](#cite_note-37)

Of the 2.3 million people displaced by the conflict since May 2013, at least 250,000 left Nigeria and fled to [Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon "Cameroon"), [Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad "Chad") or [Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger "Niger").[[36]](#cite_note-38) Boko Haram killed over 6,600 people in 2014.[[37]](#cite_note-39)[[38]](#cite_note-40) The group has carried out [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacres "Massacres") including the [killing by fire of 59 schoolboys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_2014_Buni_Yadi_massacre "February 2014 Buni Yadi massacre") in February 2014 and mass [abductions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidnapping_in_Nigeria "Kidnapping in Nigeria") including the [kidnapping of 276 schoolgirls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chibok_schoolgirls_kidnapping "Chibok schoolgirls kidnapping") in [Chibok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chibok "Chibok"), [Borno State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borno_State "Borno State"), Nigeria, in April 2014. [Corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Nigeria "Corruption in Nigeria") and [human rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights "Human rights") abuses in the security services have hampered efforts to [counter the unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterinsurgency "Counterinsurgency").[[39]](#cite_note-41)[[40]](#cite_note-hrw_report-42)

In mid-2014, the militants gained control of swaths of territory in and around their home [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_of_Nigeria "States of Nigeria") of Borno, estimated at 50,000 square kilometres (20,000 sq mi) in January 2015, but did not capture the state capital, [Maiduguri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maiduguri "Maiduguri"), where the group was originally based.[[41]](#cite_note-43) On 7 March 2015, Boko Haram's leader [Abubakar Shekau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Shekau "Abubakar Shekau") pledged allegiance to the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant "Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant"). According to the [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC"), due to [internal disputes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intragroup_conflict "Intragroup conflict") between the two groups, hundreds of militants left Boko Haram and formed their own organisation, named "Islamic State's West Africa Province".[[42]](#cite_note-longwarjournal_2016-10-44)[[43]](#cite_note-theatlantic_387235-45)[[44]](#cite_note-46) In September 2015, the director of information at the Defence Headquarters of Nigeria announced that all Boko Haram camps had been destroyed but attacks from the group continue.[[45]](#cite_note-defeat-47) In 2019, the president of Nigeria, [Muhammadu Buhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammadu_Buhari "Muhammadu Buhari"), claimed that Boko Haram was "technically defeated".[[46]](#cite_note-nytimes20190913-48) Shekau was killed and confirmed to be dead in May 2021.[[47]](#cite_note-shekaudead-49) Despite this, Boko Haram experienced a subsequent revival under a new leader, [Bakura Doro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakura_Doro "Bakura Doro").[[48]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisis_Group20241%E2%80%932,_4%E2%80%935-50)

## Name

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: Name")]

The organisation's name has been Group of the People of [Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah "Sunnah") for [Dawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawah "Dawah") and [Jihad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihad "Jihad") (Arabic: جماعة أهل السنة للدعوة والجهاد, romanised:_Jamā'atu Ahli is-Sunnah lid-Da'wati wal-Jihād_).[[49]](#cite_note-51) It was also known as the West African Province (_Wilayat Garb Ifrqiya_), and, after pledging allegiance to [Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State "Islamic State") in 2015, was briefly called [Islamic State in West Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_in_West_Africa "Islamic State in West Africa") (ISWA) or [Islamic State's West African Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State's_West_African_Province "Islamic State's West African Province") (ISWAP). The group fractured in 2016, however, and ISWAP and Boko Haram are now separate groups.[[50]](#cite_note-Britannica-52)

The name _Boko Haram_ is usually translated as "[Western education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_education "Western education") is forbidden". "Haram" is from the Arabic حَرَام (_[ḥarām](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E1%B8%A4ar%C4%81m "Ḥarām")_, "forbidden") and the [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_language "Hausa language") word _boko_ (the first vowel is long, the second pronounced in a low tone), meaning "fake",[[51]](#cite_note-53)[[52]](#cite_note-George_Percy_Bargery_1934-54) a word used to refer to secular Western education.[[53]](#cite_note-boko-55) In a 2009 statement they denounced that translation as the work of the "infidel media", claiming the true translation is "Western Civilization is forbidden", and that they are not "opposed to formal education coming from the West" but "believe in the supremacy of Islamic culture (not education)".[[54]](#cite_note-56)[[55]](#cite_note-Thurston-2019-16-57) Other translations in English include "Western influence is a sin",[[56]](#cite_note-58) and "Westernization is sacrilege".[[24]](#cite_note-enc-26) Until the death of its founder [Mohammed Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Yusuf_(Boko_Haram) "Mohammed Yusuf (Boko Haram)"), the group was also reportedly known as _Yusifiyya_. Northern Nigerians have commonly dismissed Western education as _ilimin boko_ ("fake education") and secular schools as _makaranta boko_ ("fake school").[[52]](#cite_note-George_Percy_Bargery_1934-54)[[53]](#cite_note-boko-55)[[57]](#cite_note-59)[[58]](#cite_note-Dr._Aliyu_U._Tilde-60)

### Causes/contributors

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Causes/contributors")]

#### Economic

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Economic")]

Some analysts have emphasised [economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Nigeria "Economy of Nigeria") causes as a factor in Boko Haram's success.[[59]](#cite_note-arguments-2011-61) Wealth in Nigeria has been concentrated among members of a small political elite.[[59]](#cite_note-arguments-2011-61)[[60]](#cite_note-WJD-62) Nigeria is Africa's biggest economy, but 60% of its population of 173 million (as of 2013)[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit) live on less than $1 a day.[[61]](#cite_note-63)[[62]](#cite_note-64)[[63]](#cite_note-65)

#### Religious

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: Religious")]

The [sharia law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia_law "Sharia law") imposed by local authorities, beginning with [Zamfara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zamfara "Zamfara") in January 2000 and covering 12 northern states by late 2002, may have promoted links between Boko Haram and [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Nigeria "Politics of Nigeria") leaders, but was considered by the group to have been corrupted.[[64]](#cite_note-journals-66)[[65]](#cite_note-67)[[66]](#cite_note-68)

#### Extant resentment of colonialism

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Extant resentment of colonialism")]

Academic Atta Barkindo explains the group's "baffling" ability to "maintain momentum" in part by the "accumulated and unaddressed grievances" against [colonialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonialism "Colonialism") in the region, including the colonial boundaries (of Nigeria, [Ghana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghana "Ghana"), etc.) established by Europeans that bear no resemblance to "pre-colonial [empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire "Empire"), ethnic or cultural territories", and by the group's use of the "historical narrative" of the Islamic [Kanem–Bornu Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanem%E2%80%93Bornu_Empire "Kanem–Bornu Empire").[[67]](#cite_note-barkindo-69) Mohammad Yusuf preached that, "our land was an Islamic state before it was turned into a land of [kafir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafir "Kafir") (infidel); the current system is contrary to true Muslim beliefs".[[68]](#cite_note-70)

#### Illiteracy/lack of education

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Illiteracy/lack of education")]

In a discussion organised by the [Woodrow Wilson Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_International_Center_for_Scholars "Woodrow Wilson International Center for Scholars"), Chief [Olusegun Obasanjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olusegun_Obasanjo "Olusegun Obasanjo"), a former president of Nigeria, highlighted the low level of [literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy "Literacy") and education in the Northern parts of the country as contributing to the perpetuation of Boko Haram. According to Obasanjo, [illiterate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiterate "Illiterate") and uneducated children are more likely to be drawn in.[[69]](#cite_note-71)

### Ideology

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Ideology")]

The founder of Boko Haram, Muhammad Yusuf, was reportedly inspired by the controversial Islamic preacher Mohammed Marwa ([Maitatsine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maitatsine "Maitatsine")), who condemned the reading of any books other than the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran "Quran").[[52]](#cite_note-George_Percy_Bargery_1934-54)[[70]](#cite_note-managing-72)[[71]](#cite_note-west-73)[[72]](#cite_note-worlddefensereview.com-74) In one 2009 interview, Yusuf expressed his opposition not only to Western education, but to [the theory of evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creationism "Creationism"), a [spherical Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_Earth "Flat Earth"), and to the idea that rain comes from "[evaporation caused by the sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_cycle "Water cycle")" rather than being created and sent down directly by God.[[73]](#cite_note-bbc20090728-75)

Boko Haram opposes the [Westernisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westernization "Westernization") of Nigerian society,[[59]](#cite_note-arguments-2011-61) which it blames for "Nigeria's culture of corruption",[[24]](#cite_note-enc-26) and demands the establishment of an [Islamic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_state "Islamic state") in Nigeria. It developed into a [jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadism "Jihadism") group in 2009. As [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni "Sunni")[Salafi Jihadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_jihadism "Salafi jihadism"), the group strives to re-establish the Islamic [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate "Caliphate") and bring all peoples under its domain, doing away with modern states and [patriotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic "Patriotic") feeling towards them. After Boko Haram declared its allegiance to the Islamic State, an IS statement proclaimed "It was the rejection of nationalism that drove the _[mujahidin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen "Mujahideen")_ in Nigeria to give _[bay'ah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bay'ah "Bay'ah")_ (fealty) to the Islamic State and wage war against the Nigerian _[murtaddin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy\_in\_Islam "Apostasy in Islam")_ (apostates) fighting for the Nigerian _[taghut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taghut "Taghut")_ (idolatrous tyrant)".[[74]](#cite_note-Thurston-2019-18-76) The movement is diffuse, and fighters who are associated with it follow the [Salafi doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement#Tenets "Salafi movement").

#### Takfir

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: Takfir")]

Members' beliefs tend to be centred on strict adherence to [Wahhabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahabi "Wahabi"), which is an extremely strict form of Sunni Islam that sees many other forms of Islam as [idolatrous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatrous "Idolatrous").[[75]](#cite_note-Congressional-77)[[76]](#cite_note-cfrBackgrounder-78)[[77]](#cite_note-CTC-79)[[78]](#cite_note-80)[[79]](#cite_note-Onuoha-81)

The group has denounced [Sufis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi "Sufi") and the members of the [Shiite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiite "Shiite") sects as infidels,[[80]](#cite_note-82) and also mainstream Sunni Muslims who fail to support their jihad. This willingness to [takfir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfir "Takfir") – i.e. accuse self-professed Muslims of being [apostates from Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy_in_Islam "Apostasy in Islam") and thus subject to execution[[81]](#cite_note-too-much-2017-288-83) – is a departure from mainstream Islam but not Salafi jihadism.

An insurgent aiming to overthrow a Muslim government faces a challenge due to mainstream Islamic doctrine, which forbids the killing or enslaving of fellow Muslims, including government officials, military personnel, or ordinary Muslims who don't support the insurgency. However, by using takfir—declaring opponents as apostates—the insurgents not only bypass this prohibition but also turn the killing of these Muslims into a "religious duty". In a 18 December 2016 speech to his commanders, Shekau proclaimed that 'even if a woman is praying and fasting, once she engages in democracy I can capture her in a battle'.[[82]](#cite_note-too-much-2017-288-9-84)

Some scholars have characterised Boko Haram's ideology as a "militant Salafi-jihadist" version of Islamic ideologies which deplores secular governments, educational and legal systems, preferring a governance system in accordance with Boko Haram's understanding of Islamic law.[[83]](#cite_note-85) Many people translate the group's name as "Western education is not allowed", but according to researchers it is more than just a matter of formal education, as they also reject political spheres they see to be incompatible with their religious world, or the authority of the government itself and any norms of society.[[84]](#cite_note-86)[[85]](#cite_note-87)

Interpretations of this resilience make use of two sets of ideas, ideological and organisational. Several groups of experts point to two sets of ideas as explanations of the group's resilience, namely, ideological and organisational.[[86]](#cite_note-88) It appears that its ideology has served the movement well in helping with bisociation in situations of leadership change, military pressure and intra-factionalism, according to studies.[[87]](#cite_note-89) Other reasons suggested for recruitment have been political marginalisation, poverty, unemployment, lack of education and insecurities, as well as differences over or dissatisfaction with state institutions.[[88]](#cite_note-90)[[89]](#cite_note-91) Such issues have made the group's messages more attractive than ever in certain communities where they are operating, along with the recruitment methods the group employs. These conditions have made it more appealing to the group's messages (in some affected communities) and its recruitment practices.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

Meanwhile, another group of scholars believes Boko Haram's ideology has long changed as a result of both internal squabbles and military engagements and is still largely committed to the notion of the utopia of a religiously-based society. The movement's propaganda and recruitment techniques have been tailored to different audiences; the younger generation and local communities, and its messages have been religious, political and socio-economic.[[90]](#cite_note-92) Researchers have therefore tended to explain this persisting group recruitment with a combination of the group's ideological appeal, the problems and grievances of the people in the north-eastern Nigeria and adjacent areas, as well as the problems of governance and conflicts in these areas.

According to researchers Jacob Zenna and Zacharias Pier,[[91]](#cite_note-93)[[82]](#cite_note-too-much-2017-288-9-84)

> after 2010... Shekau, believed that jihad was obligatory and that not actively joining his jihad was tantamount to apostasy. This did not mean Shekau actively killed anyone after he announced jihad and renamed the group "JAS" in 2010. Rather, there was a "priority scale" with Christians, the government and publicly anti-JAS Muslim preachers targeted first. This also meant any Muslims killed collaterally were not a concern since they were "guilty" for not having joined his jihad.... [by] October 2010,... assassinations targeting Muslim religious leaders, especially Salafists who opposed JAS's religious interpretation, as well as civil servants, became an almost weekly occurrence in northeastern Nigeria. In addition to this, prisons, banks, mosques, churches and beer halls also were common targets of attack.

## History

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: History")]

[![Image 13](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/2/2a/Boko_Haram_logo_%282002%E2%80%9315%29.png/250px-Boko_Haram_logo_%282002%E2%80%9315%29.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Boko_Haram_logo_(2002%E2%80%9315).png)

Logo of Boko Haram (2002–15)

### Background

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section: Background")]

Further information: [Fourth Nigerian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Nigerian_Republic "Fourth Nigerian Republic")

Before it was [colonised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Nigeria#A_British_sphere_of_influence "History of Nigeria") and subsequently incorporated into the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire") as [Colonial Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Nigeria "Colonial Nigeria") in 1900, the [Bornu Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bornu_Empire "Bornu Empire") ruled the territory where Boko Haram is currently active. It was a [sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign "Sovereign")[sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate "Sultanate") run according to the principles of the [Constitution of Medina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina "Constitution of Medina"), with a majority [Kanuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanuri_people "Kanuri people") Muslim population. In 1903 the [Sokoto Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sokoto_Caliphate "Sokoto Caliphate") (and parts of the [Borno Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borno_Emirate "Borno Emirate")) had come under mostly British rule, and in the [Adamawa Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamawa_Wars "Adamawa Wars") to German rule in its eastern provinces. At this time, [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity "Christianity")[missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionary "Missionary") spread the Christian message in the region and converted a large segment of the Nigerian populace.[[92]](#cite_note-Nigeria-94) British rule ended when Nigeria was granted independence in 1960.[[93]](#cite_note-bbc20110826-95)[[94]](#cite_note-96) Except for a brief period of civilian rule between 1979 and 1983, Nigeria was governed by a series of military dictatorships from 1966 until the advent of democracy in 1999.

According to the Borno [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism "Sufism")[Imam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imam "Imam")[Sheik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheikh "Sheikh") Fatahi, Yusuf was trained by the [Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kano_(city) "Kano (city)")[Salafi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement "Salafi movement")[Izala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izala_Society "Izala Society") Sheik [Ja'afar Mahmud Adamu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ja'afar_Mahmud_Adam "Ja'afar Mahmud Adam"), who called him the "leader of young people"; the two split some time in 2002–2004. They both preached in [Maiduguri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maiduguri "Maiduguri")'s Indimi [Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosque "Mosque"), which was attended by the deputy governor of Borno.[[95]](#cite_note-97) Many of the group were reportedly inspired by Mohammed Marwa, known as [Maitatsine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maitatsine "Maitatsine") ("He who curses others"), a self-proclaimed [prophet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophet "Prophet") (_annabi_, a [Hausa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hausa_language "Hausa language") word usually used only to describe the founder of Islam) born in [Northern Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Cameroons "British Cameroons") who condemned the reading of books other than the Quran.[[52]](#cite_note-George_Percy_Bargery_1934-54)[[70]](#cite_note-managing-72)[[71]](#cite_note-west-73)[[72]](#cite_note-worlddefensereview.com-74) In a 2009 [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") interview, Yusuf expressed similarly [pre-modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-modern "Pre-modern") ideas on [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution "Evolution"), a [flat earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_earth "Flat earth"), and rain sent directly from [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God "God") rather than [evaporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evaporation "Evaporation").[[73]](#cite_note-bbc20090728-75) Followers of Maitatsine "wreaked havoc" in northern cities of Nigeria "off and on" from 1980 to 1985.[[96]](#cite_note-Thurston-2019-24-98)

[Ethnic militancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_violence "Ethnic violence") is thought to have been one of the causes of the [1967–1970 civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Civil_War "Nigerian Civil War"); [religious violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_violence_in_Nigeria "Religious violence in Nigeria") reached a new height in 1980 in Kano, the largest city in the north of the country, where the Muslim fundamentalist sect [Yan Tatsine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yan_Tatsine "Yan Tatsine") ("followers of Maitatsine") instigated [riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Kano_riot "1980 Kano riot") that resulted in four or five thousand deaths. In the ensuing military crackdown, Maitatsine was killed, fuelling a backlash of increased violence that spread across other northern cities over the next twenty years.[[97]](#cite_note-Martin_Ewi-99) Social inequality and poverty contributed both to the Maitatsine and Boko Haram uprisings.[[64]](#cite_note-journals-66):97–98

In the decades since the end of British rule, politicians and academics from the mainly Islamic North have expressed their fundamental opposition to Western education. Political ethno-religious interest groups, whose membership includes influential political, military and religious leaders, have thrived in Nigeria, though they were largely suppressed under military rule. Their [paramilitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramilitary "Paramilitary") wings, formed since the country's return to civilian rule, have been implicated in much of the sectarian violence in the years following. The [Arewa People's Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arewa_People's_Congress "Arewa People's Congress"), the militia wing of the [Arewa Consultative Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arewa_Consultative_Forum "Arewa Consultative Forum"), the main political group representing the interests of northern Nigeria, is a well-funded group with military and intelligence expertise and is considered capable of engaging in military action, including covert bombing.[[98]](#cite_note-100)

### Founding

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Founding")]

[Mohammed Yusuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Yusuf_(Boko_Haram) "Mohammed Yusuf (Boko Haram)") founded the sect that became known as Boko Haram in 2002 in Maiduguri, the capital of the north-eastern state of Borno. He established a religious complex and school that attracted poor Muslim families from across Nigeria and neighbouring countries. The centre had the political goal of creating an [Islamic state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_state "Islamic state"), and became a recruiting ground for [jihadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadism "Jihadism"). By denouncing the police and state corruption, Yusuf attracted followers from unemployed youth.[[76]](#cite_note-cfrBackgrounder-78)[[93]](#cite_note-bbc20110826-95)[[99]](#cite_note-IRIN-101)[[100]](#cite_note-econ1405-102) He is reported to have used the existing infrastructure of the [Izala Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izala_Society "Izala Society") (Jama'at Izalatil Bidiawa Iqamatus Sunnah), a popular conservative Islamic sect, to recruit members, before breaking away to form his own faction. The Izala were originally welcomed into government, along with people sympathetic to Yusuf. Boko Haram conducted its operations more or less peacefully during the first seven years of its existence, withdrawing from society into remote north-eastern areas, believing it was important to develop strength before waging jihad.[[101]](#cite_note-too-much-2017-103) The government repeatedly ignored warnings about the increasingly militant character of the organisation.[[77]](#cite_note-CTC-79)[[102]](#cite_note-accused-104) The Council of [Ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama "Ulama") advised the government and the [Nigerian Television Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Television_Authority "Nigerian Television Authority") not to broadcast Yusuf's preaching, but their warnings were ignored. Yusuf's arrest elevated him to hero status. Stephen Davis, a former Anglican clergyman who has negotiated with Boko Haram many times blames local Nigerian politicians who support local bandits like Boko Haram in order for them to make life difficult for their political opponents. In particular Davis has blamed the former governor of Borno State [Ali Modu Sheriff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali_Modu_Sheriff "Ali Modu Sheriff"), who initially supported Boko Haram, but no longer needed them after the 2007 elections and stopped funding them. Sheriff denies the accusations.[[103]](#cite_note-Lamb-105)

### Rivalry with ISIL

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Rivalry with ISIL")]

Boko Haram and ISIL were initially allies. However, the two groups became enemies due to territorial disputes, because ISIL tried to conquer the zones which were under Boko Haram's control.[[104]](#cite_note-106)

In July 2014, Shekau released a 16-minute video in which he voiced his support for [ISIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State "Islamic State")'s leader [Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi "Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi"), al-Qaeda's leader [Ayman al-Zawahiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayman_al-Zawahiri "Ayman al-Zawahiri") and the [Afghan Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taliban "Taliban")'s leader [Mullah Omar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mullah_Omar "Mullah Omar").[[105]](#cite_note-107) In March 2015, Shekau pledged allegiance to [ISIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant "Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant") self-styled [caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliph "Caliph")[Abu Bakr Al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_Al-Baghdadi "Abu Bakr Al-Baghdadi") and became "West African Province" (_Wilayat Garb Ifriqiyah_), i.e. the West African province of the Islamic State. However, in August 2016, Al-Baghdadi replaced Shekau with Abu Musab al-Barnawi, the son of the Boko Haram's previous leader Muhammad Yusuf, their motivation (at least according to one source) being to trim back Shekau's tendency to apply [takfir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takfir "Takfir") (accusations of apostasy) to "all mainstream Muslims".[[106]](#cite_note-too-much-2017-282-108)

Shortly before Boko Haram pledged allegiance to ISIL, the Nigerian government launched the [2015 West African offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_West_African_offensive "2015 West African offensive") during which Boko Haram lost most of the territory which it had occupied over time. Following this offensive, the group retreated to the [Sambisa Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambisa_Forest "Sambisa Forest") and commenced its [guerilla warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_warfare "Guerrilla warfare") tactics.

In August 2016, [ISIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State "Islamic State") attempted to remove Shekau from his leadership role and replace him with [Abu Musab al-Barnawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Musab_al-Barnawi "Abu Musab al-Barnawi"). ISIL attempted to remove Shekau because he had disobeyed [Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi "Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi")'s order to cease targeting Muslim civilians. Shekau rejected the move, leading to a split between the groups. As of 2017, there were three factions which were all Boko Haram in origin, all rejecting "democracy, secularism and Western influence", and seeking to establish an Islamic state implementing [sharia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharia "Sharia"). These were the "West African Province" which is part of [ISIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISIL "ISIL"); "Jamā'at Ahl as-Sunnah lid-Da'wah wa'l-Jihād" (Boko Haram), under Shekau's control; and "[Ansaru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansaru "Ansaru")" which is loyal to [al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda "Al-Qaeda") and rejected the caliphate of al-Bagdadi, though it shares his disapproval of the "wide-reaching interpretation of takfir" of Shekau.[[107]](#cite_note-too-much-2017-283-4-109)

Since 2018, there has been a [major spike in attacks](#2018) by Boko Haram, (concentrated in [Borno State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borno_State "Borno State")), which sought to prove Boko Haram to be the prevalent terror group in the country. Attacks by Boko Haram resulted in over 1700 fatalities in the first half of 2019, including casualties of its own members.[[108]](#cite_note-110) When climate change-induced poverty and violence struck the [Lake Chad basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad_Basin "Chad Basin"), the terrorist organisation was able to recruit in large numbers by offering small loans[[109]](#cite_note-111) and promising big rewards.[[110]](#cite_note-112) It also continues to kidnap and force young boys to join them.[[111]](#cite_note-113) Boko Haram is better equipped with drones, weapons and vehicles captured from and sometimes abandoned by Nigerian military during ambush.[[46]](#cite_note-nytimes20190913-48) To better fortify itself, Nigerian military has concentrated the rural population in its garrison towns. This has allowed Boko Haram to operate freely in the countryside. In 2019 they were believed to be back in control of 4 out of 10 zones of Borno State.[[46]](#cite_note-nytimes20190913-48)

For at least four years since the split happened, the two groups were not enemies and sometimes co-operated in some terror attacks. However, Boko Haram and ISWAP later became enemies since 2021 or even a year before. ISWAP gunmen targeted Shekau in an attack carried out on 20 May 2021. Several Boko Haram members were killed in the operation, while Shekau blew himself up, or tried to do so, in order to avoid a capture. It was the first major clash between the two groups.[[112]](#cite_note-114) His death was confirmed by his loyalists led by Sahalaba in June.[[113]](#cite_note-sahara-115)

## Campaign of violence

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: Campaign of violence")]

Further information: [Boko Haram insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boko_Haram_insurgency "Boko Haram insurgency") and [Timeline of the Boko Haram insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Boko_Haram_insurgency "Timeline of the Boko Haram insurgency")

Boko Haram's attacks consist of suicide bombings as well as conventional armed assaults on both civilian and military targets. Following the Chibok kidnapping in 2014, the majority of Boko Haram's suicide bombers are female; many are teenagers and the youngest was seven. Boko Haram jihadists rely on stealth, blending into local communities or hiding in the vast countryside. Critics accuse the Nigerian military of not properly equipping its soldiers to fight Boko Haram.[[114]](#cite_note-116)[[115]](#cite_note-117)

### 2009

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=15 "Edit section: 2009")]

[![Image 14](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/3d/Ni-map.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ni-map.png)

Map of Nigeria from the CIA _[World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_World\_Factbook "The World Factbook")_

#### Uprising

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=16 "Edit section: Uprising")]

Further information: [2009 Boko Haram uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Boko_Haram_uprising "2009 Boko Haram uprising")

In 2009, police began an investigation into the group, code-named Operation Flush.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] On 26 July, security forces arrested nine Boko Haram members and confiscated weapons and bomb-making equipment. Either this or a clash with police during a funeral procession led to revenge attacks on police and widespread rioting in [Bauchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauchi_(city) "Bauchi (city)"), Maiduguri, [Potiskum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potiskum "Potiskum") in [Yobe State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yobe_State "Yobe State") and [Wudil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wudil "Wudil") in [Kano State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kano_State "Kano State"). A joint military task force operation was launched in response. By 30 July, more than 700 people had been killed; [police stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_station "Police station"), [prisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Prisons_Service "Nigerian Prisons Service"), government offices, schools and churches had been destroyed.[[24]](#cite_note-enc-26)[[64]](#cite_note-journals-66):98–102[[116]](#cite_note-Nigeria:_Boko_Haram_101-118)[[117]](#cite_note-NOSSITER-119) Yusuf was arrested, and died in custody "while trying to escape". As had been the case decades earlier in the wake of the 1980 Kano riots, the killing of the leader of an extremist group would have unintended consequences. He was succeeded by [Abubakar Shekau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Shekau "Abubakar Shekau"), formerly his second-in-command.[[118]](#cite_note-120)[[119]](#cite_note-121)

### 2010

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section: 2010")]

#### Bauchi prison break

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=18 "Edit section: Bauchi prison break")]

Main article: [Bauchi prison break](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauchi_prison_break "Bauchi prison break")

On 7 September, having regrouped under their new leader, Boko Haram broke 105 of its members out of prison in Bauchi along with over 600 other prisoners. The group went on to intensify their insurgency, launching many attacks in Nigeria, mostly in the north of the country.[[97]](#cite_note-Martin_Ewi-99)[[120]](#cite_note-Boko_Haram_attacks_%E2%80%93_timeline-122)[[121]](#cite_note-Peace_and_Security_Council_Report-123)

#### Jos and Maiduguri attacks

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=19 "Edit section: Jos and Maiduguri attacks")]

Main article: [2010 Jos and Maiduguri attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Jos_and_Maiduguri_attacks "2010 Jos and Maiduguri attacks")

On 24 December, Boko Haram [detonated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detonate "Detonate") four bombs in Jos and attacked two churches in Maiduguri.[[122]](#cite_note-124)

#### December Abuja bombing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=20 "Edit section: December Abuja bombing")]

Main article: [December 2010 Abuja bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2010_Abuja_bombing "December 2010 Abuja bombing")

On 31 December, Boko Haram bombed a [barracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barracks "Barracks") in Abuja, killing four civilians.

### 2011

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=21 "Edit section: 2011")]

Main articles: [2011 Abuja police headquarters bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Abuja_police_headquarters_bombing "2011 Abuja police headquarters bombing") and [2011 Abuja United Nations bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Abuja_United_Nations_bombing "2011 Abuja United Nations bombing")

Under Shekau's leadership, the group continuously improved its operational capabilities. It launched a string of [IED](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Improvised_explosive_device "Improvised explosive device") attacks against [soft targets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_target "Soft target") and its first [vehicle-borne IED](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle-borne_IED "Vehicle-borne IED") attack on 16 June 2011, killing six at [Abuja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abuja "Abuja")'s police headquarters. On 26 August, Boko Haram bombed the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations") (UN) headquarters in Abuja, the first time they had struck a Western target. A spokesman claiming responsibility for the attack, in which 11 UN staff members died as well as 12 others, with more than 100 injured, warned of future planned attacks on U.S. and Nigerian government interests. Speaking soon after the U.S. embassy's announcement of the arrival in the country of the [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Bureau_of_Investigation "Federal Bureau of Investigation"), he went on to announce Boko Haram's terms for negotiation, i.e. the release of all imprisoned members. The increased sophistication of the group led observers to speculate that Boko Haram was affiliated with [Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Islamic_Maghreb "Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb") (AQIM), which was active in [Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger "Niger").[[120]](#cite_note-Boko_Haram_attacks_%E2%80%93_timeline-122)[[121]](#cite_note-Peace_and_Security_Council_Report-123)[[123]](#cite_note-125)[[124]](#cite_note-Counterterrorism_2014_Calendar-126)[[125]](#cite_note-127)[[126]](#cite_note-128)[[127]](#cite_note-Reuters-129)

Boko Haram has maintained a steady rate of attacks since 2011, striking a wide range of targets, multiple times per week. They have attacked politicians, religious leaders, security forces and civilian targets. The tactic of [suicide bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_attack "Suicide attack"), used in the two attacks in the capital – on the police and UN headquarters – was new to Nigeria. In Africa as a whole, it had only been used by [al-Shabaab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shabaab_(militant_group) "Al-Shabaab (militant group)") in [Somalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somalia "Somalia") and to a lesser extent AQIM.[[23]](#cite_note-Bureau-25)[[24]](#cite_note-enc-26)[[124]](#cite_note-Counterterrorism_2014_Calendar-126)[[128]](#cite_note-Richard_Dowden-130)[[129]](#cite_note-131)[[130]](#cite_note-132)

#### Presidential inauguration

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=22 "Edit section: Presidential inauguration")]

Main articles: [2011 Nigerian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Nigerian_presidential_election "2011 Nigerian presidential election") and [May 2011 Nigeria bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2011_Nigeria_bombings "May 2011 Nigeria bombings")

Within hours of [Goodluck Jonathan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodluck_Jonathan "Goodluck Jonathan")'s presidential inauguration on 29 May 2011, Boko Haram carried out a series of bombings in Bauchi, [Zaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaria "Zaria") and Abuja. The most successful of these was the attack on the army barracks in Bauchi. A spokesman for the group told BBC Hausa that the attack had been carried out, as a test of loyalty, by serving members of the military hoping to join the group. This charge was later refuted by an army spokesman who claimed: "This is not a [banana republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banana_republic "Banana republic")". However, on 8 January 2012 the president said that Boko Haram had infiltrated both the army and the police, as well as the executive, parliamentary and legislative branches of government. Boko Haram's spokesman also claimed responsibility for the killing outside his home in Maiduguri of the politician Abba Anas Ibn Umar Garbai, the younger brother of the Shehu of Borno, who was the second most prominent Muslim in the country after the [Sultan of Sokoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_of_Sokoto "Sultan of Sokoto"). He added: "We are doing what we are doing to fight injustice, if they stop their satanic ways of doing things and the injustices, we would stop what we are doing".[[131]](#cite_note-133)[[132]](#cite_note-134)

This was one of several political and religious [assassinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination "Assassination") Boko Haram carried out that year, with the presumed intention of correcting what they say are injustices in the group's home state of Borno. Meanwhile, the trail of [massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre "Massacre") continued relentlessly, apparently leading the country towards civil war. By the end of 2011, these conflicting strategies led observers to question the group's cohesion; comparisons were drawn with the diverse motivations of the militant factions of the oil-rich [Niger Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger_Delta "Niger Delta"). Adding to the confusion, in November the [State Security Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Security_Service_(Nigeria) "State Security Service (Nigeria)") announced that four criminal syndicates were operating under the name Boko Haram.[[128]](#cite_note-Richard_Dowden-130)[[133]](#cite_note-135)[[134]](#cite_note-JEAN_HERSKOVITS-136)[[135]](#cite_note-137)

The common theme throughout the northeast was the targeting of police, who were regularly killed at work or in [drive-by shootings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drive-by_shooting "Drive-by shooting") at their homes, either in revenge for the killing of Yusuf, or as representatives of the state apparatus, or for no particular reason. Five officers were arrested for Yusuf's murder, which had no noticeable effect on the level of unrest. Opportunities for criminal enterprise flourished. Hundreds of police were dead and more than 60 police stations had been attacked by mid-2012. The government's response to this self-reinforcing trend towards insecurity was to invest heavily in security equipment, spending $5.5 billion, 20% of their overall budget, on bomb detection units, communications and transport; and $470 million on a [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa%E2%80%93China_relations "Africa–China relations")[CCTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CCTV "CCTV") system for Abuja, which has failed in its purpose of detecting or deterring acts of [terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrorism "Terrorism").[[134]](#cite_note-JEAN_HERSKOVITS-136)[[136]](#cite_note-138)[[137]](#cite_note-139)[[138]](#cite_note-140)[[139]](#cite_note-HRW2012-141)[[140]](#cite_note-142)

The election defeat of former military dictator [Muhammadu Buhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammadu_Buhari "Muhammadu Buhari") increased ethno-religious political tensions, as it broke the terms of a tacit agreement that the presidency would alternate after two terms of office between candidates from the Christian south and Muslim north of the country. Sectarian riots engulfed the twelve northern states of the country during the three days following the election, leaving more than 800 dead and 65,000 displaced.

Main article: [November 2011 Nigeria attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/November_2011_Nigeria_attacks "November 2011 Nigeria attacks")

Main article: [Christmas 2011 Nigeria attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_2011_Nigeria_attacks "Christmas 2011 Nigeria attacks")

Main article: [December 2011 Nigeria clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2011_Nigeria_clashes "December 2011 Nigeria clashes")

The subsequent campaign of violence by Boko Haram culminated in a string of bombings across the country on Christmas Day. In the outskirts of Abuja, 37 died in a church that had its roof blown off. One resident commented, "Cars were in flames and bodies littered everywhere", a phrase commonly repeated in international press reports about the bombings. Similar Christmas events had been reported in previous years. Jonathan declared a [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_emergency "State of emergency") on New Year's Eve in local government areas of Jos, Borno, [Yobe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yobe "Yobe") and [Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger_State "Niger State") and closed the international border in the north-east.[[141]](#cite_note-143)[[142]](#cite_note-144)[[143]](#cite_note-145)[[144]](#cite_note-146)[[145]](#cite_note-147)[[146]](#cite_note-148)[[147]](#cite_note-149)[[148]](#cite_note-150)[[149]](#cite_note-FalolaHeaton2008-151)

### 2012

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=23 "Edit section: 2012")]

#### State of emergency

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section: State of emergency")]

Three days later they began a series of mostly small-scale attacks on Christians and members of the [Igbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igbo_people "Igbo people") ethnic group, causing hundreds to flee. In Kano, on 20 January, they carried out by far their most deadly action yet, an assault on police buildings, killing 190. One of the victims was a TV reporter. The attacks included a combined use of car bombs, suicide bombers and IEDs, supported by uniformed gunmen.[[75]](#cite_note-Congressional-77)[[150]](#cite_note-152)[[151]](#cite_note-MIKE_OBOH-153)[[152]](#cite_note-154)[[153]](#cite_note-155)[[154]](#cite_note-156)[[155]](#cite_note-157)

[Amnesty International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amnesty_International "Amnesty International") and [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch "Human Rights Watch") published reports in 2012 that were widely quoted by government agencies and the media, based on research conducted over the course of the conflict in the worst affected areas of the country. The [NGOs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NGO "NGO") were critical of both security forces and Boko Haram. HRW stated "Boko Haram should immediately cease all attacks, and threats of attacks, that cause loss of life, injury, and destruction of property. The Nigerian government should take urgent measures to address the human rights abuses that have helped fuel the violent militancy". According to the 2012 [US Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_Department_of_State "US Department of State")_[Country Report on Human Rights Practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United\_States'\_Country\_Reports\_on\_Human\_Rights\_Practices "United States' Country Reports on Human Rights Practices")_:[[40]](#cite_note-hrw_report-42)

> [S]erious [human rights problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Nigeria "Human rights in Nigeria") included extrajudicial killings by security forces, including [summary executions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summary_executions "Summary executions"); security force torture, rape, and other cruel, [inhuman, or degrading treatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inhuman,_or_degrading_treatment "Inhuman, or degrading treatment") of prisoners, detainees, and criminal suspects; harsh and life-threatening prison and detention centre conditions; [arbitrary arrest and detention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitrary_arrest_and_detention "Arbitrary arrest and detention"); prolonged pretrial detention; denial of fair public trial; executive influence on the judiciary; infringements on citizens' privacy rights; restrictions on freedom of speech, press, assembly, religion, and movement.
>
>
> On 9 October, witnesses in Maiduguri claimed members of the [JTF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Task_Force "Joint Task Force") "Restore Order" [a [vigilante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigilante "Vigilante") group], based in Maiduguri, went on a [killing spree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_spree "Killing spree") after a suspected Boko Haram bomb killed an officer. Media reported the JTF killed 20 to 45 civilians and razed 50 to 100 houses in the neighbourhood. The JTF commander in Maiduguri denied the allegations. On 2 November, witnesses claimed the JTF shot and killed up to 40 people during raids in Maiduguri. The army claimed it dismissed some officers from the military as a result of alleged abuses committed in Maiduguri, but there were no known formal prosecutions in Maiduguri by year's end.
>
>
> Credible reports also indicated... uniformed military personnel and [paramilitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramilitary "Paramilitary")[mobile police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Mobile_Police "Nigerian Mobile Police") carried out summary executions, assaults, torture, and other abuses throughout Bauchi, Borno, [Kano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kano_State "Kano State"), [Kaduna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaduna_State "Kaduna State"), [Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plateau_State "Plateau State"), and Yobe states... The national police, army, and other security forces committed [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing "Extrajudicial killing") and used lethal and [excessive force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excessive_force "Excessive force") to apprehend criminals and suspects, as well as to disperse protesters. Authorities generally did not hold police accountable for the use of excessive or [deadly force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deadly_force "Deadly force") or for the deaths of persons [in custody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_in_custody "Death in custody"). Security forces generally operated with impunity in the illegal apprehension, detention, and sometimes [extrajudicial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_punishment "Extrajudicial punishment")[execution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution "Execution") of criminal suspects. The reports of state or federal panels of inquiry investigating [suspicious deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suspicious_death "Suspicious death") remained unpublished.
>
>
> There were no new developments in the case of five police officers accused of executing Muhammad Yusuf in 2009 at a state police headquarters. In July 2011, authorities arraigned five police officers in the federal high court in Abuja for the [murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder "Murder") of Yusuf. The court granted bail to four of the officers, while one remained in custody.
>
>
> Police use of excessive force, including use of live ammunition, to disperse demonstrators resulted in numerous killings during the year. For example, although the January fuel subsidy demonstrations generally remained peaceful, security forces reportedly fired on protesters in various states across the country during those demonstrations, resulting in 10 to 15 deaths and an unknown number of wounded.
>
>
> Despite some improvements resulting from the closure of police checkpoints in many parts of the country, states with an increased security presence due to the activities of Boko Haram experienced a rise in violence and lethal force at police and military [roadblocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roadblock "Roadblock").
>
>
> Continuing abductions of civilians by criminal groups occurred in the Niger Delta and [Southeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biafra "Biafra")... Police and other security forces were often implicated in the kidnapping schemes.
>
>
> Although the constitution and law prohibit such practices and provide for punishment of such abuses, torture is not criminalised, and security service personnel, including police, military, and State Security Service (SSS) officers, regularly tortured, beat, and abused demonstrators, criminal suspects, detainees, and convicted prisoners. Police [mistreated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mistreatment "Mistreatment") civilians to extort money. The law prohibits the introduction into trials of evidence and confessions obtained through torture; however, police often used torture to [extract confessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_confession "Forced confession").[[156]](#cite_note-158)

### 2013

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section: 2013")]

[![Image 15](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Lakechad_map.png/250px-Lakechad_map.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lakechad_map.png)

[Lake Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Chad "Lake Chad")

Nigeria's Borno State, where Boko Haram is based, adjoins [Lake Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Chad "Lake Chad") as do [Niger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niger "Niger"), [Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon "Cameroon") and the country of [Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad "Chad"). The conflict and refugees spilled over the national borders to involve all four countries.

In 2013, Boko Haram increased operations in Northern Cameroon, and were involved in skirmishes along the borders of Chad and Niger. They were linked to a number of kidnappings, often reportedly in association with the splinter group [Ansaru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansaru "Ansaru"), drawing towards them a higher level of international attention.

The [U.S. Bureau of Counterterrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Counterterrorism "Bureau of Counterterrorism") provides the following summary of Boko Haram's 2013 foreign operations:

> In February 2013, Boko Haram was responsible for kidnapping seven French tourists in the far north of Cameroon. In November 2013, Boko Haram members kidnapped a French priest in Cameroon. In December 2013, Boko Haram gunmen reportedly attacked civilians in several areas of northern Cameroon. Security forces from Chad and Niger also reportedly partook in skirmishes against suspected Boko Haram members along Nigeria's borders. In 2013, the group also kidnapped eight French citizens in northern Cameroon and obtained ransom payments for their release.[[23]](#cite_note-Bureau-25)

Boko Haram has often managed to evade the Nigerian Army by retreating into the hills around the border with Cameroon, whose army is apparently unwilling to confront them. Nigeria, Chad and Niger had formed a Multinational Joint Task Force in 1998. In February 2012, Cameroon signed an agreement with Nigeria to establish a Joint Trans-Border Security Committee, which was inaugurated in November 2013, when Cameroon announced plans to conduct "coordinated but separate" border patrols in 2014. It convened again in July 2014 to further improve cooperation between the two countries.[[157]](#cite_note-159)[[158]](#cite_note-160)[[159]](#cite_note-161)[[160]](#cite_note-162)[[161]](#cite_note-163)

In late 2013, Amnesty International received 'credible' information that over 950 inmates had died in custody, mostly in detention centres in Maiduguri and [Damaturu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damaturu "Damaturu"), within the first half of the year. Official state corruption was also documented in December 2013 by the [UK Home Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_Office "Home Office"):[[162]](#cite_note-164)[[163]](#cite_note-165)

> The NPF [Nigeria Police Force], SSS, and military report to civilian authorities; however, these security services periodically act outside of civilian control. The government lack effective mechanisms to investigate and punish abuse and corruption. The NPF remain susceptible to corruption, commit human rights abuses, and generally operate with impunity in the apprehension, illegal detention, and sometimes execution of criminal suspects. The SSS also commit human rights abuses, particularly in restricting freedom of speech and press. In some cases private citizens or the government brought charges against perpetrators of human rights abuses in these units. However, most cases lingered in court or went unresolved after an initial investigation.

The state of emergency was extended in May 2013 to cover the whole of the three north-eastern states of [Borno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borno_State "Borno State"), [Adamawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamawa_State "Adamawa State") and [Yobe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yobe_State "Yobe State"), raising tensions in the region. In the 12 months following the announcement, 250,000 fled the three states, followed by a further 180,000 between May and August 2014. A further 210,000 fled from bordering states, bringing the total displaced by the conflict to 650,000. Many thousands left the country. An August 2014 Amnesty International video showed Army and allied militia executing people, including by slitting their throats, and dumping their corpses in mass graves.[[164]](#cite_note-166)[[165]](#cite_note-167)[[166]](#cite_note-168) According to Human Rights Watch, more than 130 villages and towns were attacked or controlled by the group.[[167]](#cite_note-169)

#### Kano shootings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section: Kano shootings")]

On 8 February, at least nine polio vaccinators were killed in shootings at two clinics in Kano.[[168]](#cite_note-170)

#### Baga massacre

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section: Baga massacre")]

See also: [2013 Baga massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Baga_massacre "2013 Baga massacre")

On 16 and 17 April, a massacre of dozens of civilians as well as the destruction of hundreds of homes and businesses occurred in [Baga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baga,_Borno "Baga, Borno"), Borno, during a battle between Boko Haram and the Nigerian Army.

#### Konduga mosque shooting

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=28 "Edit section: Konduga mosque shooting")]

Main article: [Konduga mosque shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konduga_mosque_shooting "Konduga mosque shooting")

On 11 August, Boko Haram killed 44 people in a [mass shooting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_shooting "Mass shooting") at a [mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mosque "Mosque") in [Konduga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konduga "Konduga"), Borno.[[169]](#cite_note-171)

### 2014

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section: 2014")]

#### January Maiduguri bombing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: January Maiduguri bombing")]

Main article: [January 2014 Maiduguri bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2014_Maiduguri_bombing "January 2014 Maiduguri bombing")

On 14 January, a car bombing in Maiduguri killed at least 17 people.[[170]](#cite_note-172)

#### Boarding school massacre

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: Boarding school massacre")]

Main article: [February 2014 Buni Yadi massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_2014_Buni_Yadi_massacre "February 2014 Buni Yadi massacre")

On 25 February, Boko Haram killed at least 59 males at the Federal Government College in Buni Yadi, Yobe. The school was attended by children aged 11 to 18.[[171]](#cite_note-173)

Some boys were burned alive in their dormitories while those who managed to escape the fire were shot or knifed to death. Some female students were abducted while others were threatened with death if they did not quit school and get married. All of the school's buildings were burned to the ground.[[172]](#cite_note-174)

#### Chibok kidnapping

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=32 "Edit section: Chibok kidnapping")]

Main article: [Chibok schoolgirls kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chibok_schoolgirls_kidnapping "Chibok schoolgirls kidnapping")

[![Image 16](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Michelle-obama-bringbackourgirls.jpg/250px-Michelle-obama-bringbackourgirls.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Michelle-obama-bringbackourgirls.jpg)

[Michelle Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Obama "Michelle Obama") raising public awareness of the Chibok kidnapping

On 14–15 April, Boko Haram kidnapped 276 schoolgirls from [Chibok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chibok "Chibok"), Borno. Shekau announced his intention of selling them into [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Nigeria "Slavery in Nigeria"). More than 50 escaped. The incident brought Boko Haram extended global media attention, much of it focused on the pronouncements of the [U.S. Former First Lady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Lady_of_the_United_States "First Lady of the United States")[Michelle Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelle_Obama "Michelle Obama").[[173]](#cite_note-175) Faced with condemnation for his perceived incompetence, as well as allegations from Amnesty International of state collusion, President Jonathan responded by hiring a Washington [PR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_relations "Public relations") firm.[[182]](#cite_note-184)

Parents of the missing girls and those who had escaped were kept waiting until July to meet with the president, which caused them concern. In October, the government announced the girls' imminent release, but the information proved unreliable. The announcement to the media of a peace agreement and the imminent release of all the missing girls was followed days later by a video message in which Shekau stated that no such meeting had taken place and that the girls had been "married off". The announcement to the media, unaccompanied by any evidence of the reality of the agreement, was thought by analysts to have been a political ploy by the president to raise his popularity before his confirmation of his candidacy in the [2015 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Nigerian_general_election "2015 Nigerian general election"). Earlier in the year, the girls' plight had featured on "#BringBackOurGirls" political campaign posters in the streets of the capital, which the president denied knowledge of and soon took down after news of criticism surfaced. These posters, which were interpreted, to the dismay of campaigners for the girls' recapture, as being designed to benefit from the fame of the kidnapping, had also been part of Jonathan's "pre-presidential campaign". In September, "#BringBackGoodluck2015" campaign posters again drew criticism.[[183]](#cite_note-185) The official announcement of the president's candidacy was made before cheering crowds in Abuja on 11 November.[[184]](#cite_note-186)

In February 2016, the organisations [International Alert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Alert "International Alert") and [UNICEF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNICEF "UNICEF") published a study revealing that girls and women released from Boko Haram captivity often face rejection upon returning to their communities and families, in part due to a culture of stigma around sexual violence.[[185]](#cite_note-187)

#### UN Sanctions

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=33 "Edit section: UN Sanctions")]

On 22 May 2014, Boko Haram was sanctioned by the UNSC's Al-Qaida Sanctions Committee.[[186]](#cite_note-188)

#### EU Sanctions

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=34 "Edit section: EU Sanctions")]

The EU placed Boko Haram on its list of designated terrorist organization on 28/29 May 2014 through Decision 483/2014.[[187]](#cite_note-:0-189)

#### May Buni Yadi attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=35 "Edit section: May Buni Yadi attack")]

Main article: [May 2014 Buni Yadi attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2014_Buni_Yadi_attack "May 2014 Buni Yadi attack")

On 27 May, soldiers, police and civilians were killed in Buni Yadi.

#### July Maiduguri bombing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: July Maiduguri bombing")]

Main article: [July 2014 Maiduguri bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2014_Maiduguri_bombing "July 2014 Maiduguri bombing")

On 1 July, a van bombing in Maiduguri killed at least 56 people.

#### Battle of Konduga

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=37 "Edit section: Battle of Konduga")]

Main article: [Battle of Konduga (2014)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Konduga_(2014) "Battle of Konduga (2014)")

On 12 September, the Nigerian Armed Forces won a battle against Boko Haram.

#### Kano attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=38 "Edit section: Kano attack")]

Main article: [2014 Kano attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Kano_attack "2014 Kano attack")

On 28 November, over 120 Muslim worshippers were killed at the [central mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Kano "Great Mosque of Kano") in Kano during Friday prayers.

#### Northern Cameroon

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: Northern Cameroon")]

[![Image 17](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Boko_Haram_vehicles_destroyed_by_Cameroon_in_Dec._2018.jpg/250px-Boko_Haram_vehicles_destroyed_by_Cameroon_in_Dec._2018.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Boko_Haram_vehicles_destroyed_by_Cameroon_in_Dec._2018.jpg)

Vehicles used by Boko Haram destroyed in Northern Cameroon

### Boko Haram continued to increase its presence in northern Cameroon. On 16 May, ten Chinese workers were abducted in a raid on a construction company camp in Waza, near the Nigerian border. Vehicles and explosives were also taken in the raid, and one Cameroonian soldier was killed. Cameroon's anti-terrorist Rapid Intervention Battalion attempted to intervene but were vastly outnumbered.[[188]](#cite_note-190) In July, the deputy prime minister's home village was attacked by around 200 militants; his wife was kidnapped, along with the Sultan of [Kolofata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolofata "Kolofata") and his family. At least 15 people, including soldiers and police, were killed in the raid. The deputy prime minister's wife was subsequently released in October, along with 26 others including the ten Chinese construction workers who had been captured in May; authorities made no comment about any ransom, which the Cameroon government had previously claimed it never pays.[[189]](#cite_note-191) In a separate attack, nine bus passengers and a soldier were shot dead and the son of a local chief was kidnapped. Hundreds of local youths are suspected to have been recruited. In August, the remote Nigerian border town of [Gwoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwoza "Gwoza") was overrun and held by the group. In response to the increased militant activity, the Cameroonian president sacked two senior military officers and sent his army chief with 1000 reinforcements to the northern border region.[[190]](#cite_note-192)[[191]](#cite_note-193)[[192]](#cite_note-194)

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=40 "Edit section: Boko Haram continued to increase its presence in northern Cameroon. On 16 May, ten Chinese workers were abducted in a raid on a construction company camp in Waza, near the Nigerian border. Vehicles and explosives were also taken in the raid, and one Cameroonian soldier was killed. Cameroon's anti-terrorist Rapid Intervention Battalion attempted to intervene but were vastly outnumbered.[188] In July, the deputy prime minister's home village was attacked by around 200 militants; his wife was kidnapped, along with the Sultan of Kolofata and his family. At least 15 people, including soldiers and police, were killed in the raid. The deputy prime minister's wife was subsequently released in October, along with 26 others including the ten Chinese construction workers who had been captured in May; authorities made no comment about any ransom, which the Cameroon government had previously claimed it never pays.[189] In a separate attack, nine bus passengers and a soldier were shot dead and the son of a local chief was kidnapped. Hundreds of local youths are suspected to have been recruited. In August, the remote Nigerian border town of Gwoza was overrun and held by the group. In response to the increased militant activity, the Cameroonian president sacked two senior military officers and sent his army chief with 1000 reinforcements to the northern border region.[190][191][192]")]

Between May and July 2014, 8,000 Nigerian refugees arrived in the country, up to 25% suffering from acute malnutrition. Cameroon, which ranked 150 out of 186 on the 2012 [UNDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNDP "UNDP")[HDI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index "Human Development Index"), hosted as of August 2014 107,000 refugees fleeing unrest in the [CAR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Republic "Central African Republic"), a number that was expected to increase to 180,000 by the end of the year.[[193]](#cite_note-195)[[194]](#cite_note-196)[[195]](#cite_note-197) A further 11,000 Nigerian refugees crossed the border into Cameroon and Chad during August.[[196]](#cite_note-198)

Further information: [December 2014 Cameroon clashes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2014_Cameroon_clashes "December 2014 Cameroon clashes")

In the second half of December, the focus of activity switched to the [Far North Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_North_Region_(Cameroon) "Far North Region (Cameroon)") of Cameroon, beginning on the morning of 17 December when an army convoy was attacked with an IED and ambushed by hundreds of militants near the border town of [Amchide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amchide "Amchide"), 60 kilometres (40 mi) north of the state capital [Maroua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maroua "Maroua"). One soldier was confirmed dead, and an estimated 116 militants were killed in the attack, which was followed by another attack overnight with unknown casualties.[[197]](#cite_note-199) On 22 December, the Rapid Intervention Battalion followed up with an attack on a Boko Haram training camp near Guirdivig, arresting 45 militants and seizing 84 children aged 7–15 who were undergoing training, according to a statement from Cameroon's [Ministry of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ministries_of_Cameroon "List of ministries of Cameroon"). The militants fled in pick-up trucks carrying an unknown number of their dead; no information on army casualties was released.[[198]](#cite_note-200) On 27–28 December, five villages were simultaneously attacked, and for the first time the Cameroon military launched air attacks when Boko Haram briefly occupied an army camp.
