Casualty figures were not released. According to Information Minister Issa Tchiroma:

> Units of the group attacked Makari, Amchide, Limani and Achigachia in a change of strategy which consists of distracting Cameroonian troops on different fronts, making them more vulnerable in the face of the mobility and unpredictability of their attacks.[[199]](#cite_note-201)

#### Expansion of occupied territory

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=41 "Edit section: Expansion of occupied territory")]

[![Image 18](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Attentat_Nyanya_VOA2.jpg/250px-Attentat_Nyanya_VOA2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Attentat_Nyanya_VOA2.jpg)

Wounded people following a [bomb attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2014_Nyanya_bombing "April 2014 Nyanya bombing") by Boko Haram in [Nyanya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyanya "Nyanya"), in April 2014

The attack on Gwoza signalled a change in strategy for Boko Haram, as the group continued to capture territory in north-eastern and eastern areas of Borno, as well as in Adamawa and Yobe. Attacks across the border were repelled by the Cameroon military.[[200]](#cite_note-202) The territorial gains were officially denied by the Nigerian military. In a video obtained by the news agency [AFP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agence_France-Presse "Agence France-Presse") on 24 August, Shekau announced that Gwoza was now part of an Islamic [caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate "Caliphate").[[201]](#cite_note-203) The town of [Bama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bama,_Nigeria "Bama, Nigeria"), 70 kilometres (45 mi) from the state capital Maiduguri, was reported to have been captured at the beginning of September, resulting in thousands of residents fleeing to Maiduguri, even as residents there were themselves attempting to flee.[[202]](#cite_note-204) The military continued to deny Boko Haram's territorial gains, which were, however, confirmed by local vigilantes who had managed to escape. The militants were reportedly killing men and teenage boys in the town of over 250,000 inhabitants. Soldiers refused orders to advance on the occupied town; hundreds fled across the border into Cameroon, but were promptly repatriated. Fifty-four [deserters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deserter "Deserter") were later sentenced to [death by firing squad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_by_firing_squad "Death by firing squad").[[203]](#cite_note-205)[[204]](#cite_note-Monica_Mark-206)

On 17 October, the [Chief of the Defence Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Defence_Staff_(Nigeria) "Chief of the Defence Staff (Nigeria)") announced that a ceasefire had been brokered, stating: "I have accordingly directed the service chiefs to ensure immediate compliance with this development in the field". Despite a lack of confirmation from the militants, the announcement was publicised in newspaper headlines worldwide. However, within 48 hours the same publications were reporting that Boko Haram attacks had continued unabated. It was reported that factionalisation would make such a deal particularly difficult to achieve.[[205]](#cite_note-207)[[206]](#cite_note-208)[[207]](#cite_note-209)

[![Image 19](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Nigeria-karte-politisch-adamawa.png/250px-Nigeria-karte-politisch-adamawa.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nigeria-karte-politisch-adamawa.png)

Location of the town of [Mubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mubi_(town) "Mubi (town)") within [Adamawa State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adamawa_State "Adamawa State")

On 29 October, [Mubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mubi_(town) "Mubi (town)"), a town of 200,000 in Adamawa, fell to the militants, further undermining confidence in the peace talks. Thousands fled south to Adamawa's capital city, [Yola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yola,_Nigeria "Yola, Nigeria").[[208]](#cite_note-210) Amid media speculation that the ceasefire announcement had been part of President Jonathan's re-election campaign, a video statement released by Boko Haram through the normal communication channels via AFP on 31 October stated that no negotiations had in fact taken place.[[209]](#cite_note-211)[[210]](#cite_note-212) Mubi was said to have been recaptured by the army on 13 November. On the same day, Boko Haram seized Chibok, but two days later the army recaptured the largely deserted town. As of 16 November it was estimated that more than twenty towns and villages had been taken control of by the militants.[[211]](#cite_note-213)[[212]](#cite_note-214) There were 27 Boko Haram attacks during the November, killing at least 786.[[213]](#cite_note-215)[[214]](#cite_note-216)

On 3 December, it was reported that several towns in North Adamawa had been recovered by the Nigerian military with the help of local vigilantes and Bala Nggilari, the [governor of Adamawa state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Governors_of_Adamawa_State "List of Governors of Adamawa State"), said that the military were aiming to recruit 4,000 vigilantes.[[215]](#cite_note-BBC20141203-217) On 13 December, Boko Haram attacked the village of Gumsuri in Borno, killing over 30 and kidnapping over 100 women and children.[[204]](#cite_note-Monica_Mark-206)

### 2015

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=42 "Edit section: 2015")]

#### Cameroon bus attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=43 "Edit section: Cameroon bus attack")]

Main article: [2015 Cameroon bus attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Cameroon_bus_attack "2015 Cameroon bus attack")

On 1 January, Boko Haram killed at least 15 people on a bus in the Far North Region of Cameroon.

#### Baga massacre

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=44 "Edit section: Baga massacre")]

Main article: [2015 Baga massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Baga_massacre "2015 Baga massacre")

On 3 January, Boko Haram attacked [Baga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baga,_Nigeria "Baga, Nigeria"), seizing it and the multinational joint task force military base. As the militants advanced the army fled. Some residents managed to escape to Chad.[[216]](#cite_note-218) Although the death toll of the massacre was earlier estimated by [Western media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_media "Western media") to be upwards of 2000,[[217]](#cite_note-219) the Defence Ministry dismissed these claims as "speculation and conjecture", estimating the figure to be closer to 150.[[218]](#cite_note-220) On 25 January, the militants advanced to [Monguno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monguno "Monguno"), capturing the town and a nearby military base. Their advance on Maiduguri and [Konduga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konduga "Konduga"), 40 km to the southeast, was repelled. After retaking Monguno, the army expelled the militants from Baga on 21 February.[[219]](#cite_note-221)

The Baga massacre was one of the Nigerian Army's biggest defeats in terms of loss of equipment and civilian casualties. Several officers were [court-martialed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court-martial "Court-martial"). In October, General [Enitan Ransome-Kuti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enitan_Ransome-Kuti "Enitan Ransome-Kuti") was dismissed from the army and sentenced to six months imprisonment. It was determined that he had failed in his duty to launch a counter-attack after retreating from the town.[[220]](#cite_note-222)

#### West African offensive

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=45 "Edit section: West African offensive")]

Main article: [2015 West African offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_West_African_offensive "2015 West African offensive")

[![Image 20](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Wilayat_al_Sudan_al_Gharbi.png/330px-Wilayat_al_Sudan_al_Gharbi.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Wilayat_al_Sudan_al_Gharbi.png)

Map of Boko Haram's territorial control on 10 April 2015, over 2 months after the start of the 2015 West African offensive

Starting on 23 January, a coalition of military forces from Nigeria, Chad, Cameroon, and Niger began a campaign against Boko Haram.[[221]](#cite_note-223) On 4 February, the [Chadian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_of_Chad "Military of Chad") killed over 200 Boko Haram militants.[[222]](#cite_note-224) Soon afterwards, Boko Haram carried out [a massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fotokol_massacre "Fotokol massacre") in the town of [Fotokol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fotokol "Fotokol") in Far North, Cameroon, killing 81 civilians, 13 Chadian soldiers and 6 Cameroonian soldiers.[[223]](#cite_note-225) On 2 March, the Nigerian military defeated Boko Haram in the [Battle of Konduga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Konduga_(2015) "Battle of Konduga (2015)").

On 7 March, Boko Haram's leader Abubakar Shekau pledged allegiance to ISIL via an audio message posted on the organisation's [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter") account.[[43]](#cite_note-theatlantic_387235-45)[[28]](#cite_note-BokoHaramIS2-30) Nigerian army spokesperson Sami Usman Kukasheka said the pledge was a sign of weakness and that Shekau was like a "drowning man".[[224]](#cite_note-226) On 12 March 2015, ISIL's spokesman [Abu Mohammad al-Adnani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Mohammad_al-Adnani "Abu Mohammad al-Adnani") released an audiotape in which he welcomed the pledge of allegiance, and described it as an expansion of the group's caliphate to West Africa.[[27]](#cite_note-BokoHaramIS-29)

On 24 March, residents of [Damasak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damasak "Damasak"), Borno State, said that Boko Haram had taken more than 400 women and children from the town as they fled from coalition forces [who retook it](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Damasak "Battle of Damasak")[and discovered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damasak_massacre "Damasak massacre") a [mass grave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_grave "Mass grave") of Boko Haram victims.[[225]](#cite_note-227) On 27 March, the Nigerian Army captured Gwoza, which was believed to be the location of Boko Haram headquarters.[[226]](#cite_note-228) On election day 28 March 2015, Boko Haram extremists killed 41 people, including a legislator, to discourage hundreds from voting.[[227]](#cite_note-229)

In March, Boko Haram lost control of the Northern Nigerian towns of [Bama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bama,_Nigeria "Bama, Nigeria")[[228]](#cite_note-BBCTide-230) and Gwoza[[229]](#cite_note-231) to the Nigerian army. The Nigerian authorities said that they had taken back 11 of the 14 districts previously controlled by Boko Haram.[[228]](#cite_note-BBCTide-230) In April, four Boko Haram camps in the [Sambisa Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambisa_Forest "Sambisa Forest") were overrun by the Nigerian military who freed nearly 300 females.[[230]](#cite_note-232) Boko Haram forces were believed to have retreated to the [Mandara Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandara_Mountains "Mandara Mountains"), along the Nigeria-Cameroon border.[[231]](#cite_note-233)

#### Damaturu, Potiskum and Kano bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=46 "Edit section: Damaturu, Potiskum and Kano bombings")]

Main article: [February 2015 Nigeria bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_2015_Nigeria_bombings "February 2015 Nigeria bombings")

In late February, suicide bombers killed about 50 people in Damaturu, Potiskum and Kano.

#### Attrition of Catholic diocese of Maiduguri

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=47 "Edit section: Attrition of Catholic diocese of Maiduguri")]

A report by the Catholic diocese of Maiduguri estimated that as of May 2015 over 5,000 Nigerian Catholics had been killed by Boko Haram. The diocese also reported 7,000 widows and 10,000 orphans among its laity. Furthermore, Boko Haram militants had taken over several parish centres within the diocese.[[232]](#cite_note-234)

#### N'Djamena suicide bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=48 "Edit section: N'Djamena suicide bombings")]

Main article: [2015 N'Djamena bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_N'Djamena_bombings "2015 N'Djamena bombings")

On 15 June, two suicide bombings of police sites in [N'Djamena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N'Djamena "N'Djamena"), the capital and largest city of Chad, killed 38 people. Boko Haram later claimed responsibility for these attacks.[[233]](#cite_note-juneattack-235) On 27 June, suicide bombers killed six members of their own cell and five police officers during a police raid.[[234]](#cite_note-236) On 11 July, a male suicide bomber disguised in a woman's burqa detonated his explosives belt in the main market of N'Djamena, next to the main mosque, killing 15 people and injuring 80.[[235]](#cite_note-237) Several days after the bombing, Boko Haram claimed responsibility via Twitter, signing as "Islamic State, West Africa province".[[236]](#cite_note-238)

#### Monguno bombing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=49 "Edit section: Monguno bombing")]

A large sack containing [homemade bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homemade_bomb "Homemade bomb")[exploded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Explode "Explode") in [Monguno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monguno "Monguno"), [Borno State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borno_State "Borno State"), Nigeria, killing at least 12 people,[[237]](#cite_note-G-239) and possibly as many as 63.[[238]](#cite_note-A-240) It happened at a camp which the Boko Haram had abandoned.[[237]](#cite_note-G-239)[[238]](#cite_note-A-240) They also carried out major attacks in Monguno [in September 2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2015_Borno_State_bombings "September 2015 Borno State bombings")[and June 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Monguno_and_Nganzai_massacres "2020 Monguno and Nganzai massacres").

#### July mosques massacres

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=50 "Edit section: July mosques massacres")]

Main article: [July 2015 Kukawa massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2015_Kukawa_massacre "July 2015 Kukawa massacre")

Boko Haram militants attacked multiple mosques on 1 and 2 July. Forty-eight men and boys were killed on the 1st at one mosque in [Kukawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kukawa "Kukawa"). Seventeen were wounded in the attack. Ninety-seven others, mostly men, were killed in numerous mosques on the 2nd with a number of women and young girls killed in their homes. An unknown number were wounded.[[239]](#cite_note-241)

#### 5 July attacks

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=51 "Edit section: 5 July attacks")]

Main article: [5 July 2015 Nigeria attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5_July_2015_Nigeria_attacks "5 July 2015 Nigeria attacks")

On 5 July, major attacks occurred in Potiskum, Jos and Borno State.[[240]](#cite_note-242)

#### Fotokol bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=52 "Edit section: Fotokol bombings")]

On 12 July, two female suicide bombers wearing burqas killed 13 people in Fotokol. In response, the governor of Far North banned the garments to prevent further similar attacks.[[241]](#cite_note-243)

#### Claims of defeat

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=53 "Edit section: Claims of defeat")]

The [March 2015 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Nigerian_general_election "2015 Nigerian general election") was won by Buhari, who had vowed to remove inefficiency and corruption in the military.[[242]](#cite_note-244) On 9 September 2015, the director of information at the Defence Headquarters, Colonel Rabe Abubakar announced that all known Boko Haram camps and cells had been destroyed, and that the group was so weakened that they could no longer hold any territory:

> These terrorists have been subdued, even if they are adopting other means and as they are re-strategising, we are also doing the same and pre-empting them. We have coordinated the air and ground assaults to make sure that these terrorists' hideouts are completely decimated. As I am speaking to you, all the terrorists' camps have completely been wiped out. So right now they are completely in disarray, have no command and control of where to plan. We have even taken over their camps that most of them abandoned and are attempting to blend into towns and communities. We have also apprehended some of them and very soon innocent Nigerians can move back to their communities. We are making a lot of headway, so people should know that Boko Haram is no longer strong enough to hold grounds. Very soon this issue of whether they are in control of any territory in Nigeria or not will come to the open. I am assuring you that they will never again recapture the territory taken from them because what is happening right now with the deployment of troops, equipment and morale will ensure that.[[45]](#cite_note-defeat-47)[[243]](#cite_note-245)

Buhari later reiterated in December that Boko Haram was "technically defeated"[[244]](#cite_note-BBCDefeated-246) and declared in December 2016 that the group had been entirely ousted from its last stronghold of Sambisa Forest.[[245]](#cite_note-247)

#### Borno

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=54 "Edit section: Borno")]

Main article: [September 2015 Borno State bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September_2015_Borno_State_bombings "September 2015 Borno State bombings")

On 20 September, a series of bombings occurred in Maiduguri and Monguno and the attacks followed an announcement by Shekau refuting the army's claims of defeat. A military spokesman stated that the event showed the "high level of desperation" of Boko Haram.[[246]](#cite_note-248) The Arewa Consultative Forum released a statement condemning the bombings and commending the military offensive:

> The ACF condemns in strong terms the continued use of suicide bombers by Boko Haram terrorists to kill innocent people in the name of a religious war, as no religion condones such cruel and barbaric act. The ACF wishes to commend the military and other security agencies for the continued onslaught on the terrorists' enclaves and hideouts, thereby dislodging them from their strong holds. The ACF urges the military not to be deterred by the cowardly act of the Boko Haram terrorists, as their renewed effort and determination will soon end the insurgency. The ACF also appeals to the military to intensify its synergy of sharing intelligence with the community.[[247]](#cite_note-249)

On 21 October in [Nganzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nganzai "Nganzai"), Borno, according to a civilian vigilante, fleeing militants shot at four cars, killing the passengers, and burnt and looted the nearby village.[[248]](#cite_note-250) On 23 October, a suicide bombing occurred in a pre dawn attack at a mosque in Maiduguri. The National Emergency Management Agency (NEMA) put the death toll at 6 while hospital sources reported 19 deaths and a vigilante claimed to have counted 28 corpses and two suicide bombers.[[249]](#cite_note-251) On the following day, four female suicide bombers claimed one victim after they were intercepted by the JTF in Maiduguri, according to a NEMA spokesman.[[250]](#cite_note-252)

On 27 October, a military operation freed 192 children and 138 women being held captive in two camps in the [Sambisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambisa "Sambisa") forest and 30 militants were killed, according to a social media statement from the Defense HQ.[[251]](#cite_note-253)[[252]](#cite_note-254) None of the captives were those taken in Chibok in April 2014.[[253]](#cite_note-general-255)

On 25 December, gunmen set fire to the village of Kimba, killing at least 14, according to vigilantes.[[254]](#cite_note-256) On 27 December, gunmen armed with [RPGs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket-propelled_grenade "Rocket-propelled grenade") battled with troops for two hours in Aldawari village in the outskirts of Maiduguri, according to NEMA. On the following morning, a bombing at a nearby mosque killed around 20, according to NEMA.[[255]](#cite_note-257)[[256]](#cite_note-258)[[257]](#cite_note-259)[[258]](#cite_note-Dec28-260)

#### Federal Capital Territory/Nasarawa

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=55 "Edit section: Federal Capital Territory/Nasarawa")]

Two bombings on 2 October that killed 18 and wounded 41, one in [Nyanya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyanya "Nyanya") in [Nasarawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasarawa_State "Nasarawa State") and the other in [Kuje](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuje "Kuje"), FCT were also claimed by Boko Haram.[[259]](#cite_note-261)

#### Adamawa

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=56 "Edit section: Adamawa")]

On 1 October, villagers in Kirchinga, Adamawa complained of a lack of security personnel after 5 residents had their throats slit during an unchallenged early morning attack. The village borders Cameroon and the Sambisa forest.[[260]](#cite_note-262) On 18 October the village of Dar, Adamawe was attacked. Maina Ularamu, a former chairman of [Madagali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madagali "Madagali") Local Government Area, stated: "A large number of gunmen invaded the village, forcing residents to flee to a nearby bush. Two female suicide bombers disguised as fleeing villagers detonated explosives in the bush where many people were hiding, killing 12 persons".[[261]](#cite_note-263) On 20 October, there were reports of a military ambush in Madagali, assisted by vigilantes, in which over 30 militants were killed.[[262]](#cite_note-264) On 21 October, according to vigilante reports a joint operation in Madagali and Gwoza killed 150 militants and rescued 36 captives.[[263]](#cite_note-265) On 23 October, a suicide bomb at a crowded mosque killed 27 in Yola, Adamawa's capital.[[264]](#cite_note-Chicago-266) On 17 November, an explosion at a food market in Yola killed 32,[[265]](#cite_note-267) in the first Nigerian bombing since 23 October attacks in Maiduguri and Yola.[[266]](#cite_note-268) On the morning of 28 December, two female suicide bombers detonated their explosives at a crowded market in Madagali. According to a local resident, at least 28 were killed.[[258]](#cite_note-Dec28-260)

#### Yobe

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=57 "Edit section: Yobe")]

On 7 October in Damaturu, Yobe at least 15 people were killed by 3 suicide bombers.[[267]](#cite_note-269) In Goniri, Yobe, seven soldiers and over 100 militants were killed, and a large arms cache was found, according to an army spokesman, who said that the recent apparent rise in suicide bombings was an indication of the success of military operations.[[268]](#cite_note-270)[[269]](#cite_note-271)

#### Kano

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=58 "Edit section: Kano")]

Boko Haram claimed responsibility for a suicide attack on a procession of Shi'ite Muslims killing at least 21, on 30 November, near the village of Dakozoye. A week earlier two bombers had killed at least 14 in Kano city.[[270]](#cite_note-272)

#### Cameroon

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=59 "Edit section: Cameroon")]

Main article: [January 2015 raid on Kolofata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_2015_raid_on_Kolofata "January 2015 raid on Kolofata")

On 12 January, Boko Haram attacked a Cameroonian military base in [Kolofata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolofata "Kolofata"), a [commune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communes_of_Cameroon "Communes of Cameroon") in the Far North region. Government forces report killing 143 militants, while one Cameroonian soldier was killed.[[271]](#cite_note-273) On 18 January, Boko Haram raided two Tourou Cameroon area villages, torching houses, killing some residents and kidnapping between 60 and 80 people including an estimated 50 young children between the ages of 10 and 15.[[272]](#cite_note-274)

On 11 October, in the far north region of Cameroon two female suicide bombers killed nine people in the town of Mora. On 18 October 10 militants were killed when they attacked a Cameroon military anti-terrorist division convoy close to the border, after a military vehicle became stuck in mud. One army commander later died of his wounds.[[273]](#cite_note-275) On 12 October, the first 90 of a proposed deployment of 300 US troops arrived in the region to assist with training, reconnaissance and airborne intelligence using [Predator drones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predator_drones "Predator drones").[[274]](#cite_note-276) On 16 October, more than six security vehicles were transferred to the Cameroon military. An [AFRICOM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Africa_Command "United States Africa Command") spokesman said that increased cooperation had led them "to study the viability of ISR flights from a temporary location in Cameroon". The deployment is "totally separate and distinct" from operations in Chad and Niger, where 250 and 85 personnel, respectively, are conducting missions including [ISR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence,_surveillance_and_reconnaissance "Intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance") and training.[[275]](#cite_note-277)

On 23 October, Boko Haram fighters were driven out of Kerawa, a village of 50,000 in Kolofata. They had briefly occupied the village until the arrival of security forces. Reports of civilian casualties ranged from eight to eleven. An army spokesman claimed the militants suffered heavy casualties. The village's military base had previously been targeted by suicide bombers on 3 September, when 30 were killed.[[276]](#cite_note-278)[[277]](#cite_note-279)[[278]](#cite_note-280)[[279]](#cite_note-281)

On 9 November, two female suicide bombers killed three Nigerians during a security check in a truck full of Nigerian refugees.[[280]](#cite_note-282) On 21 November, a suicide attack in a suburb of [Fotokol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fotokol "Fotokol") town killed four. An anonymous military official said: "The first kamikaze detonated his bomb in the house of the traditional chief of Leymarie. Five people died including the bomber. Several minutes later, three female bombers exploded their bombs close to the initial site but they didn't kill anyone else because they acted too quickly".[[281]](#cite_note-283)

On 28 November, two suicide bombers killed six near the military base in Dabanga, and in an attack in Gouzoudou five people were killed, according to a military spokesman.[[282]](#cite_note-284) On 1 December, two suicide bombers killed three, and a third bomber was killed before detonating explosives. On 2 December, Cameroon's Defense Minister claimed that, at the end of November, 100 Boko Haram members had been killed and 900 hostages freed, and that a large stockpile of arms and munitions, and black-and-white ISIL flags had been seized.[[283]](#cite_note-285) Information Minister Issa Tchiroma Bakari said that "[t]he people that were freed are just villagers. The [Chibok] schoolgirls who are missing are not amongst the group".[[284]](#cite_note-286)

#### Chad - October and November

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=60 "Edit section: Chad - October and November")]

Main article: [Baga Sola bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baga_Sola_bombings "Baga Sola bombings")

On 6 October, the Chadian Army reported an attack in the border region of Lake Chad. 11 soldiers were killed and 14 wounded in the pre-dawn cross-border infiltration, and 17 militants were also killed, according to an army spokesman.[[285]](#cite_note-287) On 10 October 5 suicide bombers killed 33 people in the market in Baga Sola, a camp for Nigerian refugees.[[286]](#cite_note-288) On 1 November, two dawn attacks on army posts occurred. Eleven militants and two soldiers were killed at Kaika, and in an attempted suicide bombing at Bougouma, "Two members of Boko Haram were neutralised and a third blew himself up, wounding 11 civilians", according to a government statement.[[287]](#cite_note-289)[[288]](#cite_note-290) A state of emergency was imposed in the western Lake Chad region on 9 November, initially for 12 days, but extended by Chad's national assembly on 18 November to four months.[[289]](#cite_note-291)

#### December Chad bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=61 "Edit section: December Chad bombings")]

Main article: [December 2015 Chad suicide bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2015_Chad_suicide_bombings "December 2015 Chad suicide bombings")

On 5 December, three female suicide bombers killed about 30 at a crowded market on the island of Koulfoua in Lake Chad.[[290]](#cite_note-292)

#### Niger

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=62 "Edit section: Niger")]

Main article: [2015 Niger raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Niger_raid "2015 Niger raid")

On 6 February, Boko Haram assaulted [Bosso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosso,_Niger "Bosso, Niger") and [Diffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffa "Diffa"). On 25 September, at least 15 civilians were massacred and stores were looted in a cross-border raid on a Niger village, according to anonymous military sources.[[291]](#cite_note-293) On 2 October two soldiers died and four were wounded in a Boko Haram attack on a village near the Nigerian border in Niger's [Diffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffa_Region "Diffa Region") province. The militants also looted stores, according to Niger army officers. On 4 October, according to an aid worker, a policeman and five civilians were killed by 4 suicide bombers near the Nigerian border. On 6 October, three suspected Boko Haram militants accidentally blew themselves up while transporting explosives to [Bosso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosso,_Niger "Bosso, Niger") town in Diffa. On 21 October, near Diffa town, two soldiers were killed by explosives while intercepting an attack. Diffa region hosts over 150,000 Nigerian refugees. It is under a state of emergency. On 14 October a curfew and movement restrictions were imposed.[[292]](#cite_note-294) At least 57 attacks occurred there from February to October. More than 1,100 Boko Haram suspects were arrested in Niger during 2015.[[293]](#cite_note-295)[[294]](#cite_note-296)[[295]](#cite_note-297)

On 11 November, two Niger military officials described an attack on a village in Bosso district in which five civilians and 20 militants were killed. A senior government official later denied that the attack had occurred, according to Reuters.[[296]](#cite_note-298)[[297]](#cite_note-299) On 26 November, Boko Haram launched a cross-border night raid on Wogom village in Diffa province. A government spokesman, Justice Minister Marou Amadou stated: "Eighteen villagers were killed, including the chief imam for the village whose throat was slit by his own nephew".[[298]](#cite_note-300)[[299]](#cite_note-301) On 13 March 2018, the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations") Special Rapporteur on the human rights of [internally displaced persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internally_displaced_person "Internally displaced person") (IDP), Cecilia Jimenez-Damary said "Since the first attacks in Niger by Boko Haram in 2015, the [Diffa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffa_Region "Diffa Region") region, in the south-eastern part of the country, has been confronted with a continuing security crisis which has uprooted more than 129,000 internally from their homes, in addition to the arrival of 108,000 refugees from Nigeria, and has triggered a humanitarian crisis.[[300]](#cite_note-302) He said that IDPs in Niger are posing huge challenges to the country and require a strong and comprehensive response. He also said situation in the regions bordering [Mali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mali "Mali") has led to the displacement of some 1,540 persons.

### 2016

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=63 "Edit section: 2016")]

#### Bodo bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=64 "Edit section: Bodo bombings")]

Main article: [2016 Bodo bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Bodo_bombings "2016 Bodo bombings")

On 25 January, over 30 people were killed by four Boko Haram suicide bombers in [Bodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bodo,_Cameroon "Bodo, Cameroon"), Far North Region, Cameroon.[[301]](#cite_note-303)

#### Dalori attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=65 "Edit section: Dalori attack")]

Main article: [2016 Dalori attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Dalori_attack "2016 Dalori attack")

On 30 January, at least 86 people were killed and at least 62 more injured in an attack by Boko Haram militants on Dalori Village which is located in Borno State, 4 kilometers from [Maiduguri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maiduguri "Maiduguri").[[302]](#cite_note-304) The [Nigerian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Army "Nigerian Army") was unable to fight the militants until reinforcements arrived, causing Boko Haram to retreat.[[303]](#cite_note-aljazeera-305)

#### Dikwa suicide bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=66 "Edit section: Dikwa suicide bombings")]

Main article: [Dikwa suicide bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dikwa_suicide_bombings "Dikwa suicide bombings")

On 9 February, two young Boko Haram female suicide bombers killed at least 60 people at an [internally displaced persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internally_displaced_person "Internally displaced person") camp in [Dikwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dikwa "Dikwa"), Borno.

#### Maiduguri bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=67 "Edit section: Maiduguri bombings")]

Main article: [2016 Maiduguri suicide bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Maiduguri_suicide_bombings "2016 Maiduguri suicide bombings")

On 16 March, two female suicide bombers killed 22 people in Maiduguri. On 29 October, two female suicide bombers killed seven people in the same city.

#### Weakening and split

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=68 "Edit section: Weakening and split")]

[Johns Hopkins University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University "Johns Hopkins University") figures indicated that Boko Haram killed 244 people in the second quarter – the lowest quarterly figure for five years.[[304]](#cite_note-306)

In early August, ISIL announced that it had appointed Abu-Musab al-Barnawi as the new leader of the group.[[305]](#cite_note-Barnawi-307) In a video released a few days later, Shekau refused to accept al-Barnawi's appointment as leader and vowed to fight him while stating that he was still loyal to ISIL's leader Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi.[[306]](#cite_note-Vows-308)

The group has since split into pro-Barnawi and pro-Shekau factions, with reports of armed clashes breaking out between them.[[307]](#cite_note-Clash-309) Shekau has released videos since the split in which he refers to his group by its previous name of Jamā'at Ahl as-Sunnah lid-Da'wah wa'l-Jihād.[[308]](#cite_note-310)

On 23 August, the Nigerian Army announced it conducted an overnight air-operation that was 'very successful' in killing the leadership of Boko Haram. Among those presumed killed was [Abubakar Shekau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abubakar_Shekau "Abubakar Shekau"), he died in an aerial bombardment in Taye village. The Nigerian Army claims that the disputed Boko Haram leader died from injuries to his shoulders while he was performing prayer.[[309]](#cite_note-311)[[310]](#cite_note-312)

On 28 August, the Nigerian president [Muhammadu Buhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammadu_Buhari "Muhammadu Buhari"), retracted the military's statement and claimed that the Boko Haram leader was 'wounded' but not killed in the air-strike.[[311]](#cite_note-313)

On 31 August, Major General Lucky Irabor stated that the militants now only controlled a few villages and towns near [Lake Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Chad "Lake Chad") and in [Sambisa Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sambisa_forest "Sambisa forest"). He further stated that the military expected recapturing the final strongholds of the group within weeks.[[312]](#cite_note-314)

#### Madagali suicide bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=69 "Edit section: Madagali suicide bombings")]

Main article: [Madagali suicide bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madagali_suicide_bombings "Madagali suicide bombings")

On 9 December, two female Boko Haram suicide bombers killed at least 57 people in [Madagali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madagali "Madagali"), Adamawa.

### 2017

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=70 "Edit section: 2017")]

[![Image 21](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b5/Boko_Haram_Amputation.png/250px-Boko_Haram_Amputation.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Boko_Haram_Amputation.png)

Boko Haram terrorists cutting the hand of a man in 2017

#### Rann bombing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=71 "Edit section: Rann bombing")]

Main article: [Rann bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rann_bombing "Rann bombing")

On 17 January, the [Nigerian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Air_Force "Nigerian Air Force") carried out an [airstrike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airstrike "Airstrike") on an IDP camp in [Rann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rann,_Borno "Rann, Borno"), Borno. They had mistaken it for a Boko Haram camp; the bombing killed dozens of civilians.

#### Increase in the number of child suicide bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=72 "Edit section: Increase in the number of child suicide bombings")]

UNICEF reported an increase in the number of child suicide bombers with 27 incidents occurring in the first three months of 2017 in Nigeria, Niger, Cameroon and Chad, compared to 30 in the entire previous year, 56 in 2015 and 4 in 2014. Kidnapped children who escape from Boko Haram are often held in custody or ostracised by their communities or families. Patrick Rose, a UNICEF regional coordinator, stated: "They are held in military barracks, separated from their parents, without medical follow-up, without psychological support, without education, under conditions and for durations that are unknown". According to the NGO: "Society's rejection of these children, and their sense of isolation and desperation, could be making them more vulnerable to promises of martyrdom through acceptance of dangerous and deadly missions".[[313]](#cite_note-315)[[314]](#cite_note-316) In addition to child suicide bombers and despite having been routed from key areas and significantly downgraded in their capacities, throughout 2016 and into 2017, Boko Haram in Nigeria continued to wage attacks against Nigerian security forces, the community-based Civilian Joint Task Forces (CJTF), and regular citizens, using improvised explosives devices (IEDs) and other crude weaponry. These were often deployed with suicide bombers; an increasing number of whom were women and girls recruited to attack markets, transportation depots, mosques, and IDP camps.[[315]](#cite_note-317)

#### Maidiguri bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=73 "Edit section: Maidiguri bombings")]

Main article: [March 2017 Maiduguri bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2017_Maiduguri_bombings "March 2017 Maiduguri bombings")

On 22 March, suicide bombers attacked an IDP camp in Maidiguri, killing four people and wounding 18 others.[[316]](#cite_note-318)

#### Mubi bombing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=74 "Edit section: Mubi bombing")]

Main article: [2017 Mubi bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Mubi_bombing "2017 Mubi bombing")

On 21 November, a suicide bomber killed 50 people inside a mosque in Mubi.

#### Diffa clash

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=75 "Edit section: Diffa clash")]

Main article: [December 2017 Diffa Region clash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2017_Diffa_Region_clash "December 2017 Diffa Region clash")

On 6 December, Boko Haram attacked Nigerian and American soldiers in Diffa Region, Niger.

### 2018

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=76 "Edit section: 2018")]

#### Dapchi kidnapping

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=77 "Edit section: Dapchi kidnapping")]

Boko Haram [kidnapped 110 schoolgirls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dapchi_schoolgirls_kidnapping "Dapchi schoolgirls kidnapping") from the Government Technical Girls College in [Dapchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dapchi "Dapchi"), Yobe State, on 19 February 2018, killing five of them on the same day. They released all but one of the surviving girls on 21 March, having been paid a large [ransom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ransom "Ransom") by the Nigerian government to do so.[[317]](#cite_note-319)[[318]](#cite_note-320)[[319]](#cite_note-321)

#### Maiduguri attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=78 "Edit section: Maiduguri attack")]

On 2 April, a Boko Haram attack on the outskirts of Maiduguri resulted in the death of 18 people and another 84 wounded. This attack came just days after the government of Nigeria claimed there was a ceasefire with Boko Haram. The attack happened in the villages of Bale Shuwa and Bale Kura, close to both Maiduguri and the city's military camp.[[320]](#cite_note-322)

#### Operation Lafiya Dole

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=79 "Edit section: Operation Lafiya Dole")]

On 18 May, the Nigerian Army killed 15 Boko Haram insurgents and rescued 49 persons in separate encounters between Boko Haram and Nigerian troops throughout the Southern Lake Chad Basin. The Nigerian troops killed 11 of the insurgents during a battle in Gamoran Village, while the remaining insurgents were killed trying to escape from the Army's efforts in Northern Borno. The Nigerian troops rescued 4 men, 33 women, and 16 children from the insurgent's hideouts in the area.[[321]](#cite_note-323)

Main article: [Operation Hadin Kai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Hadin_Kai "Operation Hadin Kai")

#### Attacks in north-east Nigeria

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=80 "Edit section: Attacks in north-east Nigeria")]

In December, Boko Haram launched a series of attacks in north-east Nigeria. Militants from Islamic State West Africa Province took over the town of Baga and seized the [Multinational Joint Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_Joint_Task_Force "Multinational Joint Task Force") base. The attacks took place two months prior to the [presidential elections in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Nigerian_general_election "2019 Nigerian general election").[[322]](#cite_note-324)

### 2019

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=81 "Edit section: 2019")]

#### Attack near Nigerian Army chief of staff's family home

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=82 "Edit section: Attack near Nigerian Army chief of staff's family home")]

On 17 January 6 Nigerian soldiers were killed and 14 injured by the Boko Haram jihadists during a raid at a village near the army chief's family home. Four military vehicles were also seized by the jihadists and two were completely destroyed.[[323]](#cite_note-325)

**Maiduguri suicide bombing**

On 16 February, Boko Haram carried out a suicide bombing in the Gwozari/Kushari district of Maiduguri resulting in 11 deaths.[[324]](#cite_note-326)[[325]](#cite_note-327)

#### Attack against Chadian security forces

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=83 "Edit section: Attack against Chadian security forces")]

On 22 March, Boko Haram militants killed at least 23 Chadian soldiers overnight, two Chadian security sources said on that day, in what appeared to be the deadliest ever such attack inside Chad by the Islamist militants. The raid occurred in the town of [Dangdala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dangdala?action=edit&redlink=1 "Dangdala (page does not exist)"), near the banks of [Lake Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Chad "Lake Chad"). One of security sources added that the assailants were believed to have crossed the border from neighbouring Niger.[[326]](#cite_note-328)

#### Konduga bombings

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=84 "Edit section: Konduga bombings")]

On 16 June, a [triple suicide bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Konduga_bombings "2019 Konduga bombings") occurred outside a television-viewing hall in Konduga, Borno.

#### Nganzai funeral attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=85 "Edit section: Nganzai funeral attack")]

On 27 July, civilians [were massacred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nganzai_funeral_attack "Nganzai funeral attack") as they returned from a funeral in [Nganzai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nganzai "Nganzai"), Borno.

#### Attack against a Nigerian military base

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=86 "Edit section: Attack against a Nigerian military base")]

On 15 August, Nigerian soldiers were killed during a gun battle with Islamist militants in a village on the outskirts of Borno capital Maiduguri.[[327]](#cite_note-329)

#### Attack against a Burkina Faso military base

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=87 "Edit section: Attack against a Burkina Faso military base")]

On 20 September, Boko Haram said that its fighters carried out an August attack in Koutougou in northern Burkina Faso that killed 24 soldiers.[[328]](#cite_note-330)

### 2020

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=88 "Edit section: 2020")]

#### Gamboru bombing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=89 "Edit section: Gamboru bombing")]

Main article: [2020 Gamboru bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Gamboru_bombing "2020 Gamboru bombing")

On 6 January, a bombing occurred at a market on a bridge in Gamboru, Borno.

#### Auno attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=90 "Edit section: Auno attack")]

On 9 February, a [massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massacre "Massacre") occurred on the A3 road in Auno, Borno.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

#### Boma attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=91 "Edit section: Boma attack")]

Main article: [March 2020 Chad and Nigeria massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_2020_Chad_and_Nigeria_massacres "March 2020 Chad and Nigeria massacres")

On 23 March, Boko Haram fighters attacked soldiers in Boma, Chad, killing 92 and destroying 24 army vehicles. It marked the deadliest ever attack by Boko Haram on the country's military forces, and a serious escalation of conflict.[[329]](#cite_note-331) In its eight-day counter-operation "Operation Bohoma Anger", Chadian army claims to have killed around one thousand Boko Haram fighters while incurring 52 casualties of its own troops.[[330]](#cite_note-332)[[331]](#cite_note-333) Of 58 suspected Boko Haram members, who had been captured during the operation, 44 died in a prison in [N'Djamena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N'Djamena "N'Djamena") mid-April 2020. The [Minister of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Justice_and_Human_Rights_(Chad) "Ministry of Justice and Human Rights (Chad)") Djimet Arabi stated that the men died as a result of poisoning.[[332]](#cite_note-334)

#### Goneri ambush

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=92 "Edit section: Goneri ambush")]

On 23 March, at least 50 Nigerian soldiers were killed in an ambush by Boko Haram fighters near Goneri village in Borno. The army stated that all Boko Haram fighters were killed, although the number was not stated.[[333]](#cite_note-335)

#### Amchide bombing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=93 "Edit section: Amchide bombing")]

On 5 April, two Boko Haram suicide bombers killed seven civilians and themselves in [Amchide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amchide "Amchide"), Far North Region, Cameroon.[[334]](#cite_note-336)

#### Gajigana attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=94 "Edit section: Gajigana attack")]

On 18 May, Just as people were preparing to break their [Ramadan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramadan "Ramadan") fast after sundown, the sect attacked the Nigerian village of Gajigana, Borno, 29 miles north of the state capital Maiduguri, killing at least 20 people and injuring 25 others. Their rebellion has now claimed more than 20,000 lives and left more than 7 million people in need of humanitarian assistance.[[335]](#cite_note-337)[[336]](#cite_note-338)

#### Gubio massacre

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=95 "Edit section: Gubio massacre")]

Main article: [Gubio massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gubio_massacre "Gubio massacre")

On 9 June, ISWAP killed 81 villagers in [Gubio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gubio "Gubio"), Borno.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

#### Monguno and Nganzai massacres

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=96 "Edit section: Monguno and Nganzai massacres")]

On 13 June in Borno, ISWAP killed at least 20 soldiers in Monguno and more than 40 civilians in Nganzai.[[337]](#cite_note-339)

#### Nguetchewe attack

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=97 "Edit section: Nguetchewe attack")]

On 2 August, Boko Haram killed at least 18 people in a [grenade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grenade "Grenade") attack at an IDP camp.[[338]](#cite_note-340)

#### Koshebe massacre

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=98 "Edit section: Koshebe massacre")]

Main article: [Koshebe massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koshebe_massacre "Koshebe massacre")

On 28 November, Boko Haram killed about 110 farmers in Koshebe, Borno.

#### Kankara kidnapping

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=99 "Edit section: Kankara kidnapping")]

Main article: [Kankara kidnapping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kankara_kidnapping "Kankara kidnapping")

On 11 December, more than 330 students were abducted from the Government Science Secondary School in [Kankara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kankara "Kankara"), [Katsina State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katsina_State "Katsina State"), after gunmen with assault rifles attacked their school. Boko Haram later claimed responsibility for this.[[339]](#cite_note-341)

#### Christmas Eve attack in Pemi

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=100 "Edit section: Christmas Eve attack in Pemi")]

Main article: [Pemi attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pemi_attack "Pemi attack")

On 24 December, Boko Haram killed at least 11 people, burnt a church and kidnapped a priest in the predominantly Christian village of Pemi in Borno.[[340]](#cite_note-342) On 26 December, [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey "Turkey") condemned the terrorist attack, describing it as "heinous". The [Turkish ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministries_of_Turkey "Ministries of Turkey") extended its sincere condolences to the families of those who died.[[341]](#cite_note-343)

#### Wulgo forest kidnapping

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=101 "Edit section: Wulgo forest kidnapping")]

On 24 December 40 loggers were seized by Boko Haram jihadists, while 3 others were killed in the Wulgo forest near the town of Gamboru in Borno. Bodies of three loggers were found in the forest by a militia leader, and the rest were presumed kidnapped.[[342]](#cite_note-344)

### 2021

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=102 "Edit section: 2021")]

#### Maiduguri attacks

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=103 "Edit section: Maiduguri attacks")]

Main article: [2021 Maiduguri rocket attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Maiduguri_rocket_attacks "2021 Maiduguri rocket attacks")

On 23 February, Boko Haram killed 10 people in Maiduguri using [rocket-propelled grenades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocket-propelled_grenade "Rocket-propelled grenade").

#### Battle of Sambisa Forest

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=104 "Edit section: Battle of Sambisa Forest")]

Main article: [Battle of Sambisa Forest (2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sambisa_Forest_(2021) "Battle of Sambisa Forest (2021)")

On 20 May, Nigerian intelligence officials said that Boko Haram leader Abubakar Shekau died after he detonated a suicide vest in order to avoid being captured during a battle with rival Islamist militants aligned with ISIS.[[343]](#cite_note-345)[[47]](#cite_note-shekaudead-49) Internal intelligence memos backing this claim were afterwards shown to the _Wall Street Journal_.[[47]](#cite_note-shekaudead-49) Five Nigerian officials, mediators, as well as phone calls intercepted by a West African spy agency backed the report of Shekau's death.[[47]](#cite_note-shekaudead-49) After Shekau's death, Boko Haram initially fell under the command of his designated successor, a cleric named Sahalaba. However, a loyal commander named [Bakura Doro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakura_Doro "Bakura Doro") gradually gained more power and influence within the group. Bakura also succeeded in rallying the remnants of Boko Haram as well as stemming IS advances.[[344]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisis_Group20245-346)

### 2022

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=105 "Edit section: 2022")]

In March, Bakura overthrew and murdered Sahalaba, taking control of Boko Haram.[[344]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisis_Group20245-346) In the same month, Catholic Church sources in Cameroon, near the border with Nigeria, claimed that they had been attacked regularly by Boko Haram militants since September 2021. In one attack "they managed to reach [Oupaï](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Oupay "Mount Oupay") by coming through Douval. They killed two people, burned the houses and carried off clothing and small animals. Since mid-February four of the seven areas of the parish have been paralysed. We thought they wouldn't be able to reach Oupaï because it is right on top of a mountain, but we were wrong!" According to an anonymous priest, the attacks seemed to be mostly focused on obtaining supplies. "In the past they entered villages, ostentatiously yelling war cries, but recently they have come discretely, taking advantage of the full moon, to surprise people in their sleep. They kill the fathers of the family and the teenagers, especially the boys. Then they pillage the family's property and destroy everything they can't carry off."[[345]](#cite_note-347)

On 11 August five soldiers were killed during a terrorist attack in Bwari, Abuja.[[346]](#cite_note-348) The soldiers were serving with the 7 Guards Battalion, Lungi Barracks, [Maitama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abuja "Abuja") and 176 Guards Battalion, [Gwagwalada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwagwalada "Gwagwalada") in the Federal Capital Territory, Abuja Organisation.

### 2023

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=106 "Edit section: 2023")]

#### Yobe State attacks

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=107 "Edit section: Yobe State attacks")]

Main article: [2023 Yobe State attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Yobe_State_attacks "2023 Yobe State attacks")

In October 2023, Boko Haram lead one of its most horrific attacks in recent years.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]The terrorist group killed 20 mourners returning from a burial of victims of an earlier attack in north-eastern Nigeria, according to police.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] The earlier attack happened in Gurokayeya and claimed the lives of 17 individuals. According to the local police, villagers were killed after refusing to pay the illegal "harvest tax" demanded by the terrorists.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

#### Regrouping and victories over ISWAP around Lake Chad

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=108 "Edit section: Regrouping and victories over ISWAP around Lake Chad")]

Over the course of 2023, Boko Haram managed to regain considerable strength under Bakura's leadership. The group managed to expand its holdings and defeated ISWAP in a series of costly battles, taking control of many islands and shore areas of Lake Chad.[[347]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTECrisis_Group20241%E2%80%932-349)

### 2024

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=109 "Edit section: 2024")]

On 21 June, a woman detonated an IED in a suicide bombing in [Konduga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konduga "Konduga") killing at least 12.[[348]](#cite_note-350)

On 29 June, at least 32 people were killed in attacks in the northeastern Nigerian city of [Gwoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwoza "Gwoza").[[349]](#cite_note-351)

### 2025

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=110 "Edit section: 2025")]

On 5 September, more than 60 people were killed in attacks by Boko Haram in Darul Jamal, [Borno State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borno_State "Borno State"), Nigeria.[[350]](#cite_note-352)

In October 2025, Boko Haram seized the Nigerian border town of Kirawa in Borno State, burning the district head's palace, a military barracks, and dozens of homes, forcing more than 5,000 people to flee to [neighbouring Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon%E2%80%93Nigeria_border "Cameroon–Nigeria border").[[351]](#cite_note-353)

Between 5 and 8 November 2025 occurred the [Battle of Lake Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Lake_Chad "Battle of Lake Chad") between Boko Haram and ISWAP.

### 2026

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=111 "Edit section: 2026")]

[Reuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reuters "Reuters") reported that on 26 January 2026 suspected Boko Haram militants encountered Nigerian soldiers and killed 7 and captured 13 including a major. 11 soldiers escaped and made it to safety, this coming after the military started a deep offensive against Boko Haram in the North East this year. The attack occurred in the [Damasak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Damasak "Damasak") area of [Borno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borno_State "Borno State").[[352]](#cite_note-354)

## Organisation

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=112 "Edit section: Organisation")]

### Leader

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=113 "Edit section: Leader")]

Boko Haram was founded by Mohammed Yusuf who led the group from 2002 until his death in 2009. After his death, his deputy, Abubakar Shekau took control of the group and led it until his suicide at the culmination of the [Battle of Sambisa Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sambisa_Forest_(2021) "Battle of Sambisa Forest (2021)") in 2021. Their new leader is [Bakura Doro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakura_Doro "Bakura Doro").

Although Boko Haram is organised in a hierarchical structure with one overall leader, the group also operates as a [clandestine cell system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clandestine_cell_system "Clandestine cell system") using a network structure,[[353]](#cite_note-START-355) with units having between 300 and 500 fighters each.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] Estimates of the total number of fighters range between 500 and 9,000.[[354]](#cite_note-conflict-news.com-356)[[355]](#cite_note-visionofhumanity.org-p53-357)[[356]](#cite_note-358)

A notable faction which arose from Boko Haram is the [Sadiku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadiku_(Boko_Haram) "Sadiku (Boko Haram)") faction, also known as Boko Haram's **[Shiroro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiroro "Shiroro") cell**, a semi-independent faction which is more tolerant of other groups, such as local bandits and the Christian-majority [Gbagyi people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gbagyi_people "Gbagyi people") in exchange for their loyalty and alliance, although they target those who reject its version of Sharia law such as when they conducted the [2026 Kwara State attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026_Kwara_State_attacks "2026 Kwara State attacks").

### Financing

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=114 "Edit section: Financing")]

#### Kidnapping for ransom

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=115 "Edit section: Kidnapping for ransom")]

Boko Haram is said to have raised substantial sums of money by kidnapping people for [ransom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ransom "Ransom"). In 2013, Boko Haram kidnapped a family of seven French [tourists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Cameroon "Tourism in Cameroon") while they were on vacation in Cameroon and two months later, Boko Haram released the hostages along with 16 others in exchange for a ransom of [US$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_dollar "United States dollar")3.15 million.[[357]](#cite_note-Caulderwood-359)

#### Extortion

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=116 "Edit section: Extortion")]

In addition to [extortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extortion "Extortion") from local residents, Boko Haram has claimed to extort money from local state governments. A spokesman of Boko Haram claimed that Kano State governor [Ibrahim Shekarau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibrahim_Shekarau "Ibrahim Shekarau") and [Bauchi State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bauchi_State "Bauchi State") governor [Isa Yuguda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isa_Yuguda "Isa Yuguda") had paid them monthly.[[358]](#cite_note-thenation20120129-360)

### Relationship with other militant groups

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=117 "Edit section: Relationship with other militant groups")]

It has long been alleged that Boko Haram had a relationship with [al-Qaeda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda "Al-Qaeda"). In 2011, letters from Boko Haram were reportedly found in [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden "Osama bin Laden")'s compound.[[359]](#cite_note-thedailybeast.com-361)

Three weeks after the [2009 Boko Haram uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Boko_Haram_uprising "2009 Boko Haram uprising") began, [al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Islamic_Maghreb "Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb") expressed sympathy for Boko Haram.[[360]](#cite_note-nytimes.com-362) Speaking by phone to reporters in November 2012, group spokesman [Abu Qaqa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Qaqa "Abu Qaqa") said: "We are together with al-Qaeda, they are promoting the cause of Islam, just as we are doing. Therefore they help us in our struggle and we help them, too." The 2012 Reuters special report details how fighters have trained with al-Qaeda affiliates in small groups over at least 6 years.[[127]](#cite_note-Reuters-129)

According to the UN Security Council listing of Boko Haram under the al-Qaeda sanctions regime in May 2014,[[361]](#cite_note-363) the group "has maintained a relationship with [AQIM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Islamic_Maghreb "Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb") for training and material support purposes", and "gained valuable knowledge on the construction of improvised explosive devices from AQIM". The UN found that a "number of Boko Haram members fought alongside al Qaeda affiliated groups in Mali in 2012 and 2013 before returning to Nigeria with terrorist expertise". AQIM is one of al-Qaeda's regional branches, whose leader, [Abu Musab Abdel Wadoud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Musab_Abdel_Wadoud "Abu Musab Abdel Wadoud"), has sworn an oath of allegiance to al-Qaeda's senior leadership.[[362]](#cite_note-longwarjournal.org-364)

Despite its historic ties with Boko Haram, al-Qaeda central has never officially accepted Boko Haram as an affiliate. The issues which divide AQ and Boko Haram are related to the extremism of Abubakar Shekau with respect to his declaration that the entire Muslim population of Nigeria is non-Muslim. Shekau argued that it was legitimate to kill Muslim civilians based on his belief that [apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy "Apostasy") was widespread among the general population of Nigerian Muslims because they voted in elections. Al-Qaeda believes that the general Muslim population of Nigeria should be considered Muslim and based on al-Qaeda's point of view, the killing of civilians is unacceptable.[[360]](#cite_note-nytimes.com-362)[[363]](#cite_note-365)

Boko Haram has engaged in battle with IS W Africa.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]

## Response of Nigerian authorities

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=118 "Edit section: Response of Nigerian authorities")]

Until the 1990s, the Nigerian military was seen as a force for stability across the region.[[364]](#cite_note-reuters1-366) But by 2014, it was short of basic equipment, including radios and [armoured vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armoured_fighting_vehicle "Armoured fighting vehicle"). Morale was said to be low. Senior officers were allegedly skimming military procurement and budget funds which were intended to pay for the standard issue equipment which is supposed to be provided to soldiers. The country's defense budget accounted for more than a third of the country's security budget of $5.8 billion, but only 10% of this money was allocated to cover capital spending.[[364]](#cite_note-reuters1-366) A 2016 [United States Department of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Defense "United States Department of Defense") assessment stated that the Nigerian administration's response to the Boko Haram crisis was marred by "high-level corruption" but that the morale in the military had improved after several former senior government officials were arrested on corruption charges.[[75]](#cite_note-Congressional-77):9

In the summer of 2013, the Nigerian military shut down mobile phone coverage in three north-eastern Nigerian states in order to disrupt Boko Haram's communication and ability to detonate IEDs. Accounts by military insiders and data of Boko Haram incidents before, during, and after the mobile phone blackout all suggest that the shut down was 'successful' from a military-tactical point of view. However, it angered citizens who lived in the region (owing to the negative social and economic consequences of the mobile shutdown) and engendered negative opinions of the state and its new emergency policies. While citizens and organisations developed various coping and circumventing strategies, Boko Haram evolved from an open network model of insurgency to a closed centralised system, shifting the centre of its operations to the Sambisa Forest. As a consequence, Boko Haram's changing strategies fundamentally changed the dynamics of the conflict.[[365]](#cite_note-367)

In July 2014, Nigeria was estimated to have suffered the highest number of terrorist killings in the world over the past year, 3477, killed in 146 attacks.[[366]](#cite_note-368) The governor of Borno, [Kashim Shettima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashim_Shettima "Kashim Shettima"), of the opposition [ANPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_Nigeria_Peoples_Party "All Nigeria Peoples Party"), said in February 2014:[[367]](#cite_note-369)

> Boko Haram are better armed and are better motivated than our own troops. Given the present state of affairs, it is absolutely impossible for us to defeat Boko Haram.

In April 2018, the President of Nigeria, [Muhammadu Buhari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammadu_Buhari "Muhammadu Buhari"), approved a release of $1bn for the procurement of security equipment to fight insurgency and revolt in the country. This announcement came days after an attack by Boko Haram that left 18 dead in northern Nigeria.[[368]](#cite_note-370)

In September 2021, Brigadier General Bernard Onyeuko of the [Nigerian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nigerian_Armed_Forces "Nigerian Armed Forces") announced the surrender of close to 6,000 Boko Haram insurgents.[[369]](#cite_note-371)

On 15 February 2024, the Borno Government says it had cleared 500 suspects of involvement in terrorism and had them released from the Nigerian Army detention facility at Giwa Barracks, Maiduguri.[[370]](#cite_note-372)

On 12 April 2026, the Nigerian Air Force launched an airstrike targeting suspected Jihadi rebels. The airstrike hit the Jilli weekly market located in a village in Yobe State near the Borno State border. Amnesty International had reported the strike killed at least 100 people with children among the casualties. Some Nigerian officials described the strike as a misfire while the Nigerian Military declared a successful strike on a "terrorist enclave and logistics hub". The market is known to be frequented by Boko Haram Jihadis but it is unknown how many of the casualties were confirmed Boko Haram Jihadists. Following the strike, the Associated Press reported that at least 500 civilians have been killed in similar misfires.[[371]](#cite_note-373)
