### African Coalition force

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=121 "Edit section: African Coalition force")]

Further information: [Multinational Joint Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_Joint_Task_Force "Multinational Joint Task Force")

After a series of meetings over many months,[[384]](#cite_note-ngrguardiannews.com-386)[[385]](#cite_note-sunnewsonline.com-387)[[386]](#cite_note-theguardian.com-388) Cameroon's foreign minister announced on 30 November 2014 that a coalition force to fight terrorism, including Boko Haram, would soon be operational. The force would include 3,500 soldiers from [Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin "Benin"), Chad, Cameroon, Niger and Nigeria.[[387]](#cite_note-389)[[388]](#cite_note-390) Discussions between the [Economic Community of West African States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States "Economic Community of West African States") (ECOWAS) about a broader based military force were held in January 2015.[[389]](#cite_note-391)

In early February 2015, an agreement to provide 7,500 African Union troops from Benin, Cameroon, Chad, and Niger was tentatively reached. On 2 February 2015, the Nigerian Army said it had recaptured [Gamboru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamboru "Gamboru") from Boko Haram, along with the nearby towns of [Mafa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mafa "Mafa"), Mallam Fatori, [Abadam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abadam "Abadam"), and [Marte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marte,_Nigeria "Marte, Nigeria") following a joint weekend offensive by Nigerian, Chadian and Cameroonian forces.[[390]](#cite_note-392) By 6 February 2015, Chadian and Nigerian warplanes and ground troops had forced Boko Haram forces to abandon about a dozen towns and villages.[[391]](#cite_note-postpones-393) On 17 February 2015, the Nigerian military retook [Monguno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monguno "Monguno") in a coordinated air and ground assault. On 6 March 2015, the African Union endorsed the creation of a regional force of more than 8,000 troops to combat Boko Haram.[[392]](#cite_note-allies-394)

### Chinese assistance

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=122 "Edit section: Chinese assistance")]

In May 2014, [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China") offered Nigeria assistance that included satellite data, and possibly [military equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_equipment "Military equipment").[[375]](#cite_note-scmp.com-377)

### Colombian assistance

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=123 "Edit section: Colombian assistance")]

In October 2015, [Colombia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colombia "Colombia") sent a delegation of security experts to assist the Nigerian authorities and share expertise on security and counter terrorism.[[393]](#cite_note-395) In January 2016, a delegation led by [Lieutenant General Tukur Yusuf Buratai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tukur_Yusuf_Buratai "Tukur Yusuf Buratai") also visited Colombia to exchange information in regards to the war against Boko Haram.[[394]](#cite_note-396)[[395]](#cite_note-397)

### South African and post-Soviet states assistance

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=124 "Edit section: South African and post-Soviet states assistance")]

In March 2015, it was reported that Nigeria had employed hundreds of [mercenaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercenaries "Mercenaries") from [South Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Africa "South Africa") and the [former Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states "Post-Soviet states") to assist it in its effort to make gains against Boko Haram before the 28 March election.[[396]](#cite_note-398)

### French and British assistance

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=125 "Edit section: French and British assistance")]

Main article: [Operation Turus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Turus "Operation Turus")

[France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France "France") and the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom "United Kingdom"), in coordination with the United States, have sent trainers and material assistance to Nigeria to assist in the fight against Boko Haram.[[375]](#cite_note-scmp.com-377) France planned to use 3,000 troops in the region for counter-terrorism operations. Israel and Canada also pledged support.[[397]](#cite_note-399)

In 2017, the United Kingdom enforced an emergency assistance package worth $259 million. The United Kingdom has also aided Nigeria through military support and counter-terrorism training. The [British government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_the_United_Kingdom "Government of the United Kingdom") has provided training to 28,000 Nigerian military troops to aid the fight against Boko Haram. More than 40 British soldiers have also been sent on a long-term deployment to Nigeria.[[398]](#cite_note-400)

On 28 August 2018, the British government produced a press release describing the details of the newly launched partnership between the United Kingdom and Nigeria which was formed in an attempt to reduce the threat posed by Boko Haram to the citizens of both nations. The press release gave insight into the multiple methods (including community engagement and direct intervention by the Nigerian government) of preventing and reducing the impacts of attacks carried out by Boko Haram in Nigeria.

Specific details of the cooperation between the British and Nigerian governments include:

A £13 million programme to educate 100,000 children living in the conflict zone and; implementing a Nigerian crisis response mechanism to help the government respond to incidents like terror attacks and; cutting the number of new recruits joining Boko Haram by tackling the false information spread by the group to recruit new members.[[399]](#cite_note-401)

### United States responses

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=126 "Edit section: United States responses")]

In 2012, the [U.S. Department of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Department_of_State "U.S. Department of State") had an internal debate on whether to place Boko Haram on its **list of FTOs** ([Foreign Terrorist Organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_State_Department_list_of_Foreign_Terrorist_Organizations "United States State Department list of Foreign Terrorist Organizations")).[[400]](#cite_note-KesslerBoko-402) The [Bureau of Counterterrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_Counterterrorism "Bureau of Counterterrorism") leaned towards designation while the [Bureau of African Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureau_of_African_Affairs "Bureau of African Affairs") urged caution.[[400]](#cite_note-KesslerBoko-402) Officials from the [Justice Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice "United States Department of Justice"), the FBI, the CIA, and a number of members of Congress urged the State Department to designate Boko Haram as an FTO.[[401]](#cite_note-403) The Nigerian government voiced its opposition to an FTO designation, citing concerns that it would raise Boko Haram's stature and have implications for humanitarian aid in the region where Boko Haram operated.[[400]](#cite_note-KesslerBoko-402) Twenty academic experts on Nigeria signed a letter to the State Department urging it not to designate Boko Haram as an FTO, saying that it would hinder NGO efforts in the region and might legitimise the Nigerian Army's human rights abuses in its efforts to fight Boko Haram.[[400]](#cite_note-KesslerBoko-402)[[402]](#cite_note-404)[[403]](#cite_note-405)

The U.S. State Department designated Boko Haram and its offshoot [Ansaru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ansaru "Ansaru") as terrorist organisations in November 2013, citing Boko Haram's links with AQIM and its responsibility for "thousands of deaths in northeast and central Nigeria over the last several years including targeted killings of civilians".[[404]](#cite_note-Office_of_the_Spokesperson-406) The State Department also cited Ansaru's 2013 kidnapping and execution of seven international construction workers.[[404]](#cite_note-Office_of_the_Spokesperson-406) In the statement it was noted, however, "These designations are an important and appropriate step, but only one tool in what must be a comprehensive approach by the Nigerian government to counter these groups through a combination of law enforcement, political, and development efforts."[[404]](#cite_note-Office_of_the_Spokesperson-406)[[405]](#cite_note-407) The State Department had resisted earlier calls to designate Boko Haram as a terrorist group after the [2011 Abuja United Nations bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Abuja_United_Nations_bombing "2011 Abuja United Nations bombing").[[406]](#cite_note-408) The U.S. government does not believe Boko Haram is currently (2014) affiliated with al Qaeda Central, despite periodic pledges of support and solidarity from its leadership for al-Qaeda, but is particularly concerned about ties between Boko Haram and Al Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb (AQIM) (including "likely sharing funds, training, and explosive materials").[[75]](#cite_note-Congressional-77)

Efforts to cooperate in freeing the Chibok schoolgirls had faltered, largely due to mutual distrust; the infiltration of the military by Boko Haram meant that U.S. officials were wary of sharing raw intelligence data, and the Nigerian military had failed to supply information that might have aided U.S. drone flights in locating the kidnapped girls. The Nigerian government claims that Boko Haram is "the West Africa branch of the world-wide Al-Qaeda movement" with connections to [al-Shabaab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shabaab_(militant_group) "Al-Shabaab (militant group)") in [Somalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somalia "Somalia") and AQIM in Mali. The Nigerian government denies having committed human rights abuses in the conflict, and therefore oppose U.S. restrictions on arms sales, which they see as being based on the U.S. mis-application of the [Leahy Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leahy_Law "Leahy Law") due to concerns over [human rights in Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Nigeria "Human rights in Nigeria"). The U.S. had supplied the Nigerian army with trucks and equipment but had blocked the sale of [Cobra helicopters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobra_helicopter "Cobra helicopter"). In November 2014 the U.S. State department again refused to supply Cobras, citing concerns over the Nigerian military's ability to maintain and use them without endangering civilians.[[407]](#cite_note-409)[[408]](#cite_note-410)[[409]](#cite_note-411)[[410]](#cite_note-cobra-412)

On 1 December 2014, the U.S. embassy in Abuja announced that the U.S. had discontinued training a Nigerian battalion at the request of the Nigerian government. A spokesman for the U.S. state department said: "We regret premature termination of this training, as it was to be the first in a larger planned project that would have trained additional units with the goal of helping the Nigerian Army build capacity to counter Boko Haram. The U.S. government will continue other aspects of the extensive bilateral security relationship, as well as all other assistance programs, with Nigeria. The U.S. government is committed to the long tradition of partnership with Nigeria and will continue to engage future requests for cooperation and training".[[410]](#cite_note-cobra-412)

On 24 September 2015, the White House announced a military aid package for African allies fighting Boko Haram. The package included up to $45 million for training and other support for [Benin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benin "Benin"), Cameroon, Chad, Niger and Nigeria.[[411]](#cite_note-413) On 14 October 2015, the White House released a statement, in accordance with the [War Powers Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_Powers_Resolution "War Powers Resolution"), announcing the deployment of 300 troops to Cameroon to conduct airborne ISR: "These forces are equipped with weapons for the purpose of providing their own force protection and security, and they will remain in Cameroon until their support is no longer needed."[[412]](#cite_note-414)

In October 2015, General [David M. Rodriguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_M._Rodriguez "David M. Rodriguez"), the head of the [United States Africa Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Africa_Command "United States Africa Command"), reported that Boko Haram has lost territory, directly contradicting statements which were made by Boko Haram. U.S. efforts to train and share intelligence with regional military forces is credited with helping to push back against Boko Haram, but officials warn that the group remains a grave threat.[[413]](#cite_note-415)

### United Nations responses

[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Boko_Haram&action=edit&section=127 "Edit section: United Nations responses")]

In January 2019, when thousands of refugees from northeastern Nigeria were forced to return from Cameroon, despite the continuous threat to civilian lives by Boko Haram jihadists, the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations") was "extremely alarmed". "This action was totally unexpected and puts lives of thousands of refugees at risk," the [United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_High_Commissioner_for_Refugees "United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees"), [Filippo Grandi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filippo_Grandi "Filippo Grandi") said. The UNHCR appealed to Cameroon "to continue its open door and hospitable policy" and stop any more returns.[[414]](#cite_note-416) In March 2021, the UN announced it was launching a $1 billion appeal in Abuja, with the goal of providing assistance to an estimated nine million northern Nigerians in need of [humanitarian aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanitarian_aid "Humanitarian aid") because of Boko Haram's 11-year insurgency.[[415]](#cite_note-417)
